Of course, they were fond of society, and some of them got a little spoiled. But they were in no danger of becoming too conventional.
Colville did not insist. “I missed the military tonight, Mrs. Bowen,” he said. “I thought one couldn’t get through an evening in Florence without officers?”
“We have them when there is dancing,” returned Mrs. Bowen.
“Yes, but they don’t know anything but dancing,” Miss Graham broke in. “I like someone who can talk something besides compliments.”
“You are very peculiar, you know, Imogene,” urged Mrs. Bowen gently. “I don’t think our young men at home do much better in conversation, if you come to that, though.”
“Oh, *young* men, yes! They’re the same everywhere. But here, even when they’re away along in the thirties, they think that girls can only enjoy flattery. *I* should like a gentleman to talk to me without a single word or look to show that he thought I was good-looking.”
“Ah, how could he be?” Colville insinuated, and the young girl coloured.
“I mean, if I were pretty. This everlasting adulation is insulting.”
“Mr. Morton doesn’t flatter,” said Mrs. Bowen thoughtfully, turning the feather screen she held at her face, now edgewise, now flatwise, toward Colville.
“Oh no,” owned Miss Graham. “He’s a clergyman.”
Mrs. Bowen addressed herself to Colville. “You must go to hear him some day. He’s very interesting, if you don’t mind his being rather Low Church.”
Colville was going to pretend to an advanced degree of ritualism; but it occurred to him that it might be a serious matter to Mrs. Bowen, and he asked instead who was the Rev. Mr. Waters.
“Oh, isn’t he lovely?” cried Miss Graham. “There, Mrs. Bowen! Mr. Waters’s manner is what I call *truly* complimentary. He always talks to you as if he expected you to be interested in serious matters, and as if you were his intellectual equal. And he’s so *happy* here in Florence! He gives you the impression of feeling every breath he breathes here a privilege. You ought to hear him talk about Savonarola, Mr. Colville.”
“Well,” said Colville, “I’ve heard a great many people talk about Savonarola, and I’m rather glad he talked to me about American girls.”
“American girls!” uttered Miss Graham, in a little scream. “Did Mr. Waters talk to you about *girls*?”
“Yes. Why not? He was probably in love with one once.”
“Mr. Waters?” cried the girl. “What nonsense!”
“Well, then, with some old lady. Would you like that better?”
Miss Graham looked at Mrs. Bowen for permission, as it seemed, and then laughed, but did not attempt any reply to Colville.
“You find even that incredible of such pyramidal antiquity,” he resumed. “Well, it *is* hard to believe. I told him what that German said, and we agreed beautifully about another type of American girl which we said we preferred.”
“Oh! What could it be?” demanded Miss Graham.
“Ah, it wouldn’t be so easy to say right offhand,” answered Colville indolently.
Mrs. Bowen put her hand under the elbow of the arm holding her screen. “I don’t believe I should agree with you so well,” she said, apparently with a sort of didactic intention.
They entered into a discussion which is always fruitful with Americans﻿—the discussion of American girlhood, and Colville contended for the old national ideal of girlish liberty as wide as the continent, as fast as the Mississippi. Mrs. Bowen withstood him with delicate firmness. “Oh,” he said, “you’re Europeanised.”
“I certainly prefer the European plan of bringing up girls,” she replied steadfastly. “I shouldn’t think of letting a daughter of mine have the freedom I had.”
“Well, perhaps it will come right in the next generation, then; she will let her daughter have the freedom she hadn’t.”
“Not if I’m alive to prevent it,” cried Mrs. Bowen.
Colville laughed. “Which plan do you prefer, Miss Graham?”
“I don’t think it’s quite the same now as it used to be,” answered the girl evasively.
“Well, then, all I can say is that if I had died before this chance, I had lived a blessed time. I perceive more and more that I’m obsolete. I’m in my dotage; I prattle of the good old times, and the new spirit of the age flouts me. Miss Effie, do you prefer the Amer﻿—”
“No, thank you,” said her mother quickly. “Effie is out of the question. It’s time you were in bed, Effie.”
The child came with instant submissiveness and kissed her mother good night; she kissed Miss Graham, and gave her hand to Colville. He held it a moment, letting her pull shyly away from him, while he lolled back in his chair, and laughed at her with his sad eyes. “It’s past the time *I* should be in bed, my dear, and I’m sitting up merely because there’s nobody to send me. It’s not that I’m really such a very bad boy. Good night. Don’t put me into a disagreeable dream; put me into a nice one.” The child bridled at the mild pleasantry, and when Colville released her hand she suddenly stooped forward and kissed him.
“You’re so *funny*!” she cried, and ran and escaped beyond the portiere.
Mrs. Bowen stared in the same direction, but not with severity. “Really, Effie has been carried a little beyond herself.”
“Well,” said Colville, “that’s *one* conquest since I came to Florence. And merely by being funny! When I was in Florence before, Mrs. Bowen,” he continued, after a moment, “there were two ladies here, and I used to go about quite freely with either of them. They were both very pretty, and we were all very young. Don’t you think it was charming?” Mrs. Bowen coloured a lovely red, and smiled, but made no other response. “Florence has changed very much for the worse since that time. There used to be a pretty flower-girl, with a wide-flapping straw hat, who flung a heavy bough full of roses into my lap when she met me driving across the Carraja bridge. I spent an hour looking for that girl today, and couldn’t find her. The only flower-girl I could find was a fat one of fifty, who kept me fifteen minutes in Via Tornabuoni while she was fumbling away at my buttonhole, trying to poke three secondhand violets and a sickly daisy into it. Ah, youth! youth! I suppose a young fellow could have found that other flower-girl at a glance; but *my* old eyes! No, we belong, each of us, to our own generation. Mrs. Bowen,” he said, with a touch of tragedy﻿—whether real or affected, he did not well know himself﻿—in his hardiness, “what has become of Mrs. Pilsbury?”
“Mrs. Milbury, you mean?” gasped Mrs. Bowen, in affright at his boldness.
“Milbury, Bilbury, Pilsbury﻿—it’s all one, so long as it isn’t﻿—”
“They’re living in Chicago!” she hastened to reply, as if she were afraid he was going to say, “so long as it isn’t Colville,” and she could not have borne that.
Colville clasped his hands at the back of his head and looked at Mrs. Bowen with eyes that let her know that he was perfectly aware she had been telling Miss Graham of his youthful romance, and that he had now touched it purposely. “And you wouldn’t,” he said, as if that were quite relevant to what they had been talking about﻿—“you wouldn’t let Miss Graham go out walking alone with a dotard like me?”
“Certainly not,” said Mrs. Bowen.
Colville got to his feet by a surprising activity. “Goodbye, Miss Graham.” He offered his hand to her with burlesque despair, and then turned to Mrs. Bowen. “Thank you for *such* a pleasant evening! What was your day, did you say?”
“Oh, any day!” said Mrs. Bowen cordially, giving her hand.
“Do you know whom you look like?” he asked, holding it.
“No.”
“Lina Ridgely.”
The ladies stirred softly in their draperies after he was gone. They turned and faced the hearth, where a log burned in a bed of hot ashes, softly purring and ticking to itself, and whilst they stood pressing their hands against the warm fronts of their dresses, as the fashion of women is before a fire, the clock on the mantel began to strike twelve.
“Was that her name?” asked Miss Graham, when the clock had had its say. “Lina Ridgely?”
“No; that was *my* name,” answered Mrs. Bowen.
“Oh yes!” murmured the young girl apologetically.
“She led him on; she certainly encouraged him. It was shocking. He was quite wild about it.”
“She must have been a cruel girl. How *could* he speak of it so lightly?”
“It was best to speak of it, and have done with it,” said Mrs. Bowen. “He knew that I must have been telling you something about it.”
“Yes. How bold it was! A *young* man couldn’t have done it! Yes, he’s fascinating. But how old and sad he looked, as he lay back there in the chair!”
“Old? I don’t think he looked old. He looked sad. Yes, it’s left its mark on him.”
The log burned quite through to its core, and fell asunder, a bristling mass of embers. They had been looking at it with downcast heads. Now they lifted their faces, and saw the pity in each other’s eyes, and the beautiful girl impulsively kissed the pretty woman good night.

===Chinese===

到了近处，格雷厄姆小姐（Miss Graham）果然不知怎么缩小了一圈，不再那么咄咄逼人了。科尔夫尔在她面前行过礼、抬起眼来，才看出她并不那么高，也并不那么丰硕，只是具备一份女性充足的丰盈。可她反而更美了，眉眼间透着一种甜美的青春光彩，叫人心动。她若已不再是隔着客厅那一头望去的女神，那么变成一个极其可爱的少女，反倒得了许多好处；她一开口，牙齿还露出一点迷人的小小不齐，给了她最后一重魅力。
鲍恩太太同那位年轻牧师飘然走开了，埃菲却留在格雷厄姆小姐身边，好像还攥着她那只随意垂在身后裙裾间的左手。孩子脸上的神情，是对格雷厄姆小姐的崇拜，远远超过对她母亲的忠诚。
“我都开始怀疑鲍恩太太到底还带不带来您了。”她坦率地说，一声天真而紧张的轻笑，替她在科尔夫尔这里挣了好感。“她晚上刚开场就应承了。”
“她对我还要狠得多呢，格雷厄姆小姐，”科尔夫尔说，“她让我从开天辟地一直等到现在。所以一路走到您跟前，我都等出白头发来了。”他灵机一动，又添一句，“鲍恩太太头一回说要给我引见的时候，我还算是个年轻人。”
“哦，多妙！”格雷厄姆小姐欢欢喜喜地说。她的同伴迟疑了一下，也礼数周全地轻笑了一小声。她有了一个发现；她发现科尔夫尔先生是位滑稽人物。
“承蒙赏脸，我很高兴。”他说，那份一本正经骗不过她们。
“可不是嘛，”格雷厄姆小姐叹道，热情得很，“这种话，除了美国人还有谁说得出？佛罗伦萨这儿有位顶可爱的老太太，住了三十年，一天到晚说要回、从来没回了，想家想得不知如何是好，她总说：美国人也许不比别国人*好*，可他们是*不一样*的。”
“这话漂亮。他们什么都跟别人不一样，只有一样不例外——觉得自己更好。他们天生的谦虚，才拦住了这一条。”
“我不大明白您的意思。”格雷厄姆小姐迟疑了一下说。
“这个嘛，”科尔夫尔回答，“我自己也还没想清楚。我也许是说，美国人跟别人不同，在于不自视甚高；也许是说，不同在于自视得还不够高。可我方才那话听着很有警句味儿，我宁可不去细究。”
这话引得格雷厄姆小姐和埃菲小姐齐声“哦！”了一声，为他话里的用意又喜又疑。两人都不知不觉朝他身边挪近了一点。
“今晚公共餐桌上（table d'hôte）有位法国太太对我说，她一猜就着我是美国人，因为美国人说话总是敲着个性的调子。”他在谈话里这样节省材料，用得毫无顾忌，同一桩见闻或话头，常常要拿去应付一屋子的人，轮着当值。
“啊，这我不信。”格雷厄姆小姐说。
“不信什么？”
“不信美国人总是谈自己。”
“她是不是这个意思，我可说不准。一个人话里是什么意思，你永远闹不清——‘他’说不清，‘她’也说不清。”
“多吓人！”
“兴许那位法国太太的意思是，我们总是谈论别人。那也在个性的调子上。”
“可我也不信我们尽谈别人，”格雷厄姆小姐说，“反正，刚才谈‘我们’的是*她*。”
“哦，这一点她有解释：巴黎有个庞大的美国人侨民群，把法国人带坏了，教会了他们我们那套自我省察的恶习。”
“您觉得我们很爱自我省察吗？”
“您觉得呢？”
“我知道我不。我几乎从来不想自己。就算想，也总是想得太晚。麻烦就麻烦在这儿。我倒希望能多想想。可我老是在琢磨别人。他们有意思得多。”
“兴许您多了解了解自己，就不这么想了。”科尔夫尔提了一句。
“是啊，我知道他们有意思。我就觉得年轻人谁都不可能有意思。”
“那所有的小说都成什么了？满篇全是年轻人。”
“都可笑极了。我要是写小说，就挑个上了年纪的当主角——三十五岁，或者四十岁。”她看了看科尔夫尔，微微红了脸，赶忙补上：“我不信人不到那个岁数就谈得上有意思。年轻男人嘴里的话，永远那么寡淡！您记不记得海涅《旅行素描》（Pictures of Travel）里的一段：他在教堂里看见一位太太忏悔时从斗篷底下伸出来的手，他一看便知那是一只年轻的手，没享过福、也没受过苦的手——那么光洁，像朵花。读了那段我就恨自己这双手——那年我才十六岁，可我好像跟小孩子一样没经历过任何事。哦，我喜欢人*经*过一些事。您呢？”
“呃，是啊，我想我喜欢。喜欢别人经历。”
“不；您自己不喜欢经历吗？”
“说不上来；我还没经历过什么值得一提的事呢。”
格雷厄姆小姐将信将疑地看他，却仍然热烈地追下去：“喏，就说重回佛罗伦萨吧——隔了这么久再回来——我想那该多浪漫。哎呀！真希望我也是第二回来这儿。”
“只怕您就不会这么喜欢了，”科尔夫尔说，“我倒希望我是头一回来。什么事都比不过头一回。”
“您真这么想？”
“反正，在佛罗伦萨，什么都比不过头一回。”
“哦，我想象不出。我倒觉得，把旧日的情感一一召回，该是顶迷人的事。”
“是啊，要是您一叫他们就肯来。可它们跟深渊里召唤来的精灵一样犟。我围着这儿喊我的旧情感喊了一整天，连一声应答的吱吱都没讨着。”
“哦！”格雷厄姆小姐睜圆了眼睛喊，“多逗啊！”埃菲·鲍恩把秀气的小脸转过去笑，仿佛当面笑话一个人的俏皮话，有点不够厚道。
得了她们的赏识，科尔夫尔的胡话更滔滔不绝了。“不；想找回你对佛罗伦萨的旧情感，唯一的法子是向正新鲜地经历着它们的人去借。您对佛罗伦萨是怎么想的，格雷厄姆小姐？”
“我？我来了才两个月。”
“那已经迟了？”
“不，倒也未必。我到现在还时时刻刻觉得新鲜得叫人发懵。可我说不清。反正跟布法罗大不一样，这个我担保。”
“布法罗？这差别我想得出。而且也未必全是布法罗吃亏。”
“哦，您去过？”格雷厄姆小姐问，那份急切叫人感动。“您在布法罗认识人吗？”
“认识几位报界的人；我每年去纽约都打那儿过——从前是这样。那是个热闹地方。”
“是啊，热闹。”格雷厄姆小姐深情地叹道。
“布法罗的姑娘们，夜里还出来在月光底下跳舞吗？”
“什么！”
“啊，我明白了，”科尔夫尔说，皱起沉思的眉头端详她，“你们果真属于另一个时代。黑人剧团那支老歌，你不记得了。”
“不记得，”格雷厄姆小姐说，“我只记得内战打完。”
“年轻得多么神圣！”科尔夫尔说。“不过，”他又说，“我真希望那位法国太太刚才偷听了咱们的话，格雷厄姆小姐。我看她准会改主意，不再说美国人谈话净敲个性的调子了。”
“哦！”姑娘飞快地一想、认出话里的机锋，埋怨起来，“您怎么能由着我这样说话！您总不至于以为，我对谁都这么讲吧？那是因为您也是美国人，又是鲍恩太太这么老的朋友。”
“回头那位法国太太要是为这事兴师问罪，我一定这么告诉她。”科尔夫尔说。他漫不经心地朝客厅那头扫了一眼，看见年轻牧师正在向鲍恩太太辞行；其余客人全走光了。“哎呀！”他说，“我该走了。”
鲍恩太太早已疾步逼到他跟前，末后几个字全叫她听去了。“为什么？”她问。
“我疑心现在已经算明天了，而请柬只请了一天。”
“那是张全季票，”鲍恩太太说，殷勤得明快，“离明天还有半个钟头呢。我可不能放您走。都到火边来吧，咱们围着炉火坐坐。火正烧到最好的时候。”
母亲这一阵少女式的兴发，叫埃菲露出又惊又喜的俏丽神情——母亲素来沉静，相形之下这阵兴致更显得夺目——她撇下格雷厄姆小姐的手，跑上前去，把圈手椅一张张围着壁炉安顿妥帖。
科尔夫尔和鲍恩太太忽然发现，两人之间已经到了那种境地——久别重逢的人，从前见面彼此有好感、又好聚好散的，常会这样——他们比自以为的亲近得多，实在也比有理由的亲近得多。
“您的客人里头，您愿意我把哪一位献祭掉，鲍恩太太？”他问。他已经陷进一张圈椅的凹处——陷得不算太深。鲍恩太太坐在他右手一张高些的椅子里，格雷厄姆小姐在左边沙发上目不转睛地望着他，一种甜滋滋的满足感从头顶灌到脚底。“只要您吩咐一声，哪一位我也不留情。”
“可惜哪一位我也不想叫他们毁灭。”鲍恩太太答道。“您不觉得他们全都挺招人喜欢吗？”
“是，是；够招人喜欢——够招人喜欢的，我想。可他们待得太久了。一想到他们要是早点走，这半个钟头咱们本可以就这么坐在这儿，我就觉得实在难以原谅他们。”
鲍恩太太和格雷厄姆小姐在他头顶上方交换了一个眼色——一个眼色问：“我没说错吧？”另一个眼色答：“可不，他*就是*！”埃菲·鲍恩的眼睛睜大了，一眨不眨地钉在科尔夫尔身上，无声地崇拜着。
他问了问他注意到的几位客人是谁，鲍恩太太便任他拿他们开了一点玩笑：玩笑都存心极厚道。他转述了那位德国人关于美国姑娘世俗心气的话；这一条她可不许他放这么开。她说她们不比别的姑娘更俗气。当然，她们喜欢交际，有几个也有点惯坏了。可她们断没有变得太刻板的危险。
科尔夫尔没有坚持。“今晚我没见着军官们，鲍恩太太。”他说，“我还以为在佛罗伦萨，一个晚上没有军官就过不完呢？”
“有舞会才请他们。”鲍恩太太回道。
“是，可他们除了跳舞什么都不会，”格雷厄姆小姐插进来，“我喜欢能谈点恭维话以外的东西的人。”
“你可真是特别，伊莫金（Imogene），你知道，”鲍恩太太柔声劝道，“不过说到底，我看咱们国内的年轻男人，谈吐也高明不到哪儿去。”
“哦，*年轻*男人，可不是！他们到哪儿都一个样。可是在这儿，人都三十好几了，还当姑娘们只受用奉承。*我*倒希望有位先生同我说话，一个字、一个眼色都不带，不叫人看出他觉得我好看。”
“啊，那他怎么做得到呢？”科尔夫尔话里有话地说。姑娘红了脸。
“我是说，假如我好看的话。这没完没了的奉承，简直是侮辱。”
“莫顿先生（Mr. Morton）可不奉承人。”鲍恩太太若有所思地说，把手里的羽毛扇屏朝脸侧转着，一会儿侧着一道，一会儿放平一扇，转向科尔夫尔。
“哦，那倒是，”格雷厄姆小姐承认，“他是位牧师。”
鲍恩太太转向科尔夫尔。“哪天您该去听他讲道。他讲得很有意思——只要您不介意他相当的低教会派。”
科尔夫尔本想装出一副高级仪式派的架势；可他想到这对鲍恩太太兴许是件顶要紧的事，便改问沃特斯牧师（Rev. Mr. Waters）是谁。
“哦，他多可爱呀！”格雷厄姆小姐喊道。“瞧，鲍恩太太！沃特斯先生的态度，才叫*真正*的恭维。他同你说话，总当你会对正经事感兴趣，当你是他智识上的对等。而且他在佛罗伦萨这儿多*幸福*！他给你的感觉是：在这里呼吸的每一口气，他都当作一种特权。您真该听听他讲萨伏那洛拉，科尔夫尔先生。”
“这个嘛，”科尔夫尔说，“讲萨伏那洛拉的人我听得多了，他跟我谈美国姑娘，我倒觉得难得。”
“美国姑娘！”格雷厄姆小姐小小地尖叫一声。“沃特斯先生会同您谈*姑娘*？”
“谈了。怎么不可以？他多半也爱过一位。”
“沃特斯先生？”姑娘喊。“瞎说什么！”
“那好，那就是爱过哪位老太太。这样你受用些吗？”
格雷厄姆小姐看了看鲍恩太太，像是请示许可，然后笑了，可也没打算回科尔夫尔什么。
“连这一点，你都觉得落不到这般金字塔式的古物头上。”他接着说，“也的确，叫人难信。我把那位德国人的话学给他听，我们又心投意合地议定了一种我们更中意的另一类型美国姑娘。”
“哦！是哪种呢？”格雷厄姆小姐追问。
“啊，这可不能脱口就说。”科尔夫尔懒懒地答。
鲍恩太太把一只手托住执扇那只手臂的臂弯。“我看我不会这么赞成你。”她说，显然带着一点教训人的意思。
他们进入了一场在美国人中间永远多产的讨论——关于美国少女的讨论。科尔夫尔为那个旧日的民族理想辩护：少女的自由该像大陆一样辽阔，像密西西比河一样奔放。鲍恩太太以精致的坚定抵抗他。“哦，”他说，“你欧化了。”
“我确实更喜欢欧洲教养姑娘的那一套。”她坚定不移地答，“要我让我的女儿享我当年享过的那种自由，我想都不会想。”
“那么，兴许到下一代就拨正了；她会让她的女儿享她自己没享过的自由。”
“只要我还活着拦着，就不行。”鲍恩太太喊道。
科尔夫尔笑了。“您赞成哪一套，格雷厄姆小姐？”
“我看如今跟从前不完全一样了。”姑娘闪闪烁烁地答。
“那么，我只有一句话：我要是在沾着这份运气之前就死了，那也是活过了一段有福的岁月。我越来越看出自己过时了。我老糊涂了；我念叨着从前的好时光，时代的新风气笑话我。埃菲小姐，你赞成美——”
“不用了，谢谢。”她母亲赶紧说。“埃菲不在此列。你该上床了，埃菲。”
孩子立刻顺从地走过来，吻了母亲道晚安；又吻了格雷厄姆小姐，把手伸给科尔夫尔。他握着那只手停了一会儿，由她害羞地往回挣，自己往椅背里一靠，用那双忧伤的眼睛笑望着她。“我该上床的时辰也早过了，亲爱的，我还赖着不睡，不过是因为没人打发我去睡。倒不是我真这么不听话。晚安。别把我丢进一个讨厌的梦里；给我挑个好的。”孩子被这句温厚的打趣惹得扬起小脸，等科尔夫尔松开手，她忽然俯身过来吻了他一下。
“你太*逗*了！”她喊着，一溜烟跑过门帘不见了。
鲍恩太太朝同一个方向望着，倒并不严厉。“真是的，埃菲有点忘形了。”
“这个嘛，”科尔夫尔说，“这算我来到佛罗伦萨以后的*第一*场征服。还全凭逗乐！上回我在佛罗伦萨的时候，鲍恩太太，”他停了停，又接着说，“那时这儿有两位女士，我同哪一位都能自在地四处走动。两位都很美，我们都还年轻。您不觉得那很动人吗？”鲍恩太太红起一层可爱的红晕，笑了，却没有别的表示。“打那时起，佛罗伦萨败坏得厉害。从前有个漂亮的卖花姑娘，戴一顶宽檐翻卷的草帽，赶着我的车过卡拉亚桥时碰见我，会往我膝上抛一大枝沉甸甸的玫瑰。今天我花了一个钟头找那姑娘，没找着。唯一找得着的卖花姑娘是个五十岁的胖子，在托尔纳博尼街（Via Tornabuoni）上缠了我一刻钟，摸索着我的扣眼，非要往里头塞三支二手的紫罗兰和一朵蔫头蔫脑的雏菊。啊，青春！青春！换个年轻小伙子，一眼就能把先前那位卖花姑娘认出来；可*我*这双老眼！不，我们谁都属于各自的那一代。鲍恩太太，”他说，那股放肆里带着一点悲剧味——是真是假，他自己也说不清——“皮尔斯伯里太太后来怎么样了？”
“您是说米尔伯里太太？”鲍恩太太倒吸一口气，被他这份大胆吓着了。
“米尔伯里、比尔伯里、皮尔斯伯里——全是一回事，只要不是——”
“他们住在芝加哥！”她赶忙答道，像是怕他就要说出“只要不是鲍恩”来——那个她可受不住。
科尔夫尔两手扣在脑后，望着鲍恩太太，那眼神叫她明白：她方才对格雷厄姆小姐讲他青年罗曼史的事，他知道得一清二楚，而这一下他是故意点到的。“那么您就不会，”他说，仿佛这同他们方才谈的话顶相干——“您就不会让格雷厄姆小姐单独同我这么个老糊涂出去散步了？”
“当然不会。”鲍恩太太说。
科尔夫尔以出人意料的利索站起身。“再见了，格雷厄姆小姐。”他带着滑稽的绝望把手伸给她，又转向鲍恩太太。“多谢您这个*这般*愉快的夜晚！您说的是哪一天来着？”
“哦，哪天都行！”鲍恩太太热热地握出手来。
“您知道自己像谁吗？”他握着那只手问。
“不知道。”
“莉娜·里奇利（Lina Ridgely）。”
他走后，两位女士在裙裾窸窣里轻轻动了动。她们转过身，面向壁炉：一根木柴在一窝滚烫的灰烬里燃着，喃喃地、噼啪地自语。她们按女人在炉火前的习惯，把手按在裙裾烘暖的前襟上站着，壁炉架上的钟敲起十二点来。
钟敲完了，格雷厄姆小姐才问：“那是她的名字？莉娜·里奇利？”
“不；那是*我*的名字。”鲍恩太太答道。
“哦，对了！”年轻姑娘歉意地低声道。
“是她引着他；她实在是有意纵着他。不成体统。他为这事简直疯了。”
“那她准是个狠心的姑娘。他怎么能说得那么轻巧？”
“说开了，了掉了，最好。”鲍恩太太说。“他知道我方才必定对你讲了些那件事。”
“是啊。多大胆呀！*年轻*男人可做不来！是啊，他迷人。可他往椅子里一靠的样子，显得又老又伤感！”
“老？我倒不觉得他显老。他是显得伤感。是啊，那件事在他身上留了印子。”
木柴一直烧透了芯，散塌下来，成了一蓬火星刺刺的余烬。两人一直低着头望着它。这时她们抬起脸，看见彼此眼中的怜惜，那美丽的姑娘冲动地吻了那漂亮的女人，道了晚安。

## IX / 九
===Original===

The next morning Paolo, when he brought up Colville’s breakfast, brought the news that there was to be a *veglione* at the Pergola Theatre. This news revived Colville’s courage. “Paolo,” he said, “you ought to open a banking-house.” Paolo was used to being joked by foreigners who could not speak Italian very well; he smiled as if he understood.
The banker had his astute doubts of Paolo’s intelligence; the banker in Europe doubts all news not originating in his house; but after a day or two the advertisements in the newspapers carried conviction even to the banker.
When Colville went to the ladies with news of the veglione, he found that they had already heard of it. “Should you like to go?” he asked Mrs. Bowen.
“I don’t know. What do you think?” she asked in turn.
“Oh, it’s for you to do the thinking. I only know what I want.”
Imogene said nothing, while she watched the internal debate as it expressed itself in Mrs. Bowen’s face.
“People go in boxes,” she said thoughtfully; “but you would feel that a box wasn’t the same thing exactly?”
“*We* went on the floor,” suggested Colville.
“It was very different then. And, besides, Mrs. Finlay had absolutely *no* sense of propriety.” When a woman has explicitly condemned a given action, she apparently gathers courage for its commission under a little different conditions. “Of course, if we went upon the floor, I shouldn’t wish it to be known at all, though foreigners can do almost anything they like.”
“Really,” said Colville, “when it comes to that, I don’t see any harm in it.”
“And you say go?”
“I say whatever you say.”
Mrs. Bowen looked from him to Imogene. “I don’t either,” she said finally, and they understood that she meant the harm which he had not seen.
“Which of us has been so good as to deserve this?” asked Colville.
“Oh, you have all been good,” she said. “We shall go in masks and dominoes,” she continued. “Nothing will happen, and who should know us if anything did?” They had received tickets to the great Borghese ball, which is still a fashionable and desired event of the Carnival to foreigners in Florence; but their preconceptions of the veglione threw into the shade the entertainment which the gentlemen of Florence offered to favoured sojourners.
“Come,” said Mrs. Bowen, “you must go with us and help us choose our dominoes.”
A prudent woman does not do an imprudent thing by halves. Effie was to be allowed to go to the veglione too, and she went with them to the shop where they were to hire their dominoes. It would be so much more fun, Mrs. Bowen said, to choose the dresses in the shop than to have them sent home for you to look at. Effie was to be in black; Imogene was to have a light blue domino, and Mrs. Bowen chose a purple one; even where their faces were not to be seen they considered their complexions in choosing the colours. If you happened to find a friend, and wanted to unmask, you would not want to look horrid. The shop people took the vividest interest in it all, as if it were a new thing to them, and these were the first foreigners they had ever served with masks and dominoes. They made Mrs. Bowen and Imogene go into an inner room and come out for the mystification of Colville, hulking about in the front shop with his mask and domino on.
“Which is which?” the ladies both challenged him, in the mask’s conventional falsetto, when they came out.
With a man’s severe logic he distinguished them according to their silks, but there had been time for them to think of changing, and they took off their masks to laugh in his face.
They fluttered so airily about among the pendent masks and dominoes, from which they shook a ghostly perfume of old carnivals, that his heart leaped.
“Ah, you’ll never be so fascinating again!” he cried. He wanted to take them in his arms, they were both so delicious; a man has still only that primitive way of expressing his supreme satisfaction in women. “Now, which am I?” he demanded of them, and that made them laugh again. He had really put his arm about Effie.
“Do you think you will know your papa at the veglione?” asked one of the shop-women, with a mounting interest in the amiable family party.
They all laughed; the natural mistake seemed particularly droll to Imogene.
“Come,” cried Mrs. Bowen; “it’s time we should be going.”
That was true; they had passed so long a time in the shop that they did not feel justified in seriously attempting to beat down the price of their dresses. They took them at the first price. The woman said with reason that it was Carnival, and she could get her price for the things.
They went to the veglione at eleven, the ladies calling for Colville, as before, in Mrs. Bowen’s carriage. He felt rather sheepish, coming out of his room in his mask and domino, but the corridors of the hotel were empty, and for the most part dark; there was no one up but the porter, who wished him a pleasant time in as matter-of-fact fashion as if he were going out to an evening party in his dress coat. His spirits mounted in the atmosphere of adventure which the ladies diffused about them in the carriage; Effie Bowen laughed aloud when he entered, in childish gaiety of heart.
The narrow streets roared with the wheels of cabs and carriages coming and going; the street before the theatre was so packed that it was some time before they could reach the door. Masks were passing in and out; the nervous joy of the ladies expressed itself in a deep-drawn quivering sigh. Their carriage door was opened by a servant of the theatre, who wished them a pleasant veglione, and the next moment they were in the crowded vestibule, where they paused a moment, to let Imogene and Effie really feel that they were part of a masquerade.
“Now, keep all together,” said Mrs. Bowen, as they passed through the inner door of the vestibule, and the brilliantly lighted theatre flashed its colours and splendours upon them. The floor of the pit had been levelled to that of the stage, which, stripped of the scenic apparatus, opened vaster spaces for the motley crew already eddying over it in the waltz. The boxes, tier over tier, blazed with the light of candelabra which added their sparkle to that of the gas jets.
“You and Effie go before,” said Mrs. Bowen to Imogene. She made them take hands like children, and mechanically passed her own hand through Colville’s arm.
A mask in red from head to foot attached himself to the party, and began to make love to her in excellent pantomime.
Colville was annoyed. He asked her if he should tell the fellow to take himself off.
“Not on any account!” she answered. “It’s perfectly delightful. It wouldn’t be the veglione without it. Did you ever see such good acting?”
“I don’t think it’s remarkable for anything but its fervour,” said Colville.
“I should like to see *you* making love to some lady,” she rejoined mischievously.
“I will make love to you, if you like,” he said, but he felt in an instant that his joke was in bad taste.
They went the round of the theatre. “That is Prince Strozzi, Imogene,” said Mrs. Bowen, leaning forward to whisper to the girl. She pointed out other people of historic and aristocratic names in the boxes, where there was a democracy of beauty among the ladies, all painted and powdered to the same marquise effect.
On the floor were gentlemen in evening dress, without masks, and here and there ladies waltzing, who had masks but no dominoes. But for the most part people were in costume; the theatre flushed and flowered in gay variety of tint that teased the eye with its flow through the dance.
Mrs. Bowen had circumscribed the adventure so as to exclude dancing from it. Imogene was not to dance. One might go to the veglione and look on from a box; if one ventured further and went on the floor, decidedly one was not to dance.
This was thoroughly understood beforehand, and there were to be no petitions or murmurs at the theatre. They found a quiet corner, and sat down to look on.
The mask in red followed, and took his place at a little distance, where, whenever Mrs. Bowen looked that way, he continued to protest his passion.
“You’re sure he doesn’t bore you?” suggested Colville.
“No, indeed. He’s very amusing.”
“Oh, all right!”
The waltz ceased; the whirling and winding confusion broke into an irregular streaming hither and thither, up and down. They began to pick out costumes and characters that interested them. Clowns in white, with big noses, and harlequins in their motley, with flat black masks, abounded. There were some admirable grasshoppers in green, with long antennae quivering from their foreheads. Two or three Mephistos reddened through the crowd. Several knights in armour got about with difficulty, apparently burdened by their greaves and breastplates.
A group of leaping and dancing masks gathered around a young man in evening dress, with long hair, who stood leaning against a pillar near them, and who underwent their mockeries with a smile of patience, half amused, half tormented.
When they grew tired of baiting him, and were looking about for other prey, the red mask redoubled his show of devotion to Mrs. Bowen, and the other masks began to flock round and approve.
“Oh, *now*,” she said, with a little embarrassed laugh, in which there was no displeasure, “I think you may ask him to go away. But don’t be harsh with him,” she added, at a brusque movement which Colville made toward the mask.
“Oh, why should I be harsh with him? We’re not rivals.” This was not in good taste either, Colville felt. “Besides, I’m an Italian too,” he said, to retrieve himself. He made a few paces toward the mask, and said in a low tone, with gentle suggestion, “Madame finds herself a little incommoded.”
The mask threw himself into an attitude of burlesque despair, bowed low with his hand on his heart, in token of submission, and vanished into the crowd. The rest dispersed with cries of applause.
“How very prettily you did it, both of you!” said Mrs. Bowen. “I begin to believe you *are* an Italian, Mr. Colville. I shall be afraid of you.”
“You weren’t afraid of *him*.”
“Oh, he was a *real* Italian.”
“It seems to me that mamma is getting all the good of the veglione,” said Effie, in a plaintive murmur. The well-disciplined child must have suffered deeply before she lifted this seditious voice.
“Why, so I am, Effie,” answered her mother, “and I don’t think it’s fair myself. What shall we do about it?”
“I should like something to eat,” said the child.
“So should I,” said Colville. “That’s reparation your mother owes us all. Let’s make her take us and get us something. Wouldn’t you like an ice, Miss Graham?”
“Yes, an *ice*,” said Imogene, with an effect of adding, “Nothing more for worlds,” that made Colville laugh. She rose slowly, like one in a dream, and cast a look as impassioned as a look could be made through a mask on the scene she was leaving behind her. The band was playing a waltz again, and the wide floor swam with circling couples.
The corridor where the tables were set was thronged with people, who were drinking beer and eating cold beef and boned turkey and slices of huge round sausages. “Oh, how *can* they?” cried the girl, shuddering.
“I didn’t know you were so ethereal-minded about these things,” said Colville. “I thought you didn’t object to the salad at Madame Uccelli’s.”
“Oh, but at the veglione!” breathed the girl for all answer. He laughed again, but Mrs. Bowen did not laugh with him; he wondered why.
When they returned to their corner in the theatre they found a mask in a black domino there, who made place for them, and remained standing near. They began talking freely and audibly, as English-speaking people incorrigibly do in Italy, where their tongue is all but the language of the country.
“Really,” said Colville, “I think I shall stifle in this mask. If you ladies will do what you can to surround me and keep me secret, I’ll take it off a moment.”
“I believe I will join you, Mr. Colville,” said the mask near them. He pushed up his little visor of silk, and discovered the mild, benignant features of Mr. Waters.
“Bless my soul!” cried Colville.
Mrs. Bowen was apparently too much shocked to say anything.
“You didn’t expect to meet me here?” asked the old man, as if otherwise it should be the most natural thing in the world. After that they could only unite in suppressing their astonishment. “It’s extremely interesting,” he went on, “extremely! I’ve been here ever since the exercises began, and I have not only been very greatly amused, but greatly instructed. It seems to me the key to a great many anomalies in the history of this wonderful people.”
If Mr. Waters took this philosophical tone about the Carnival, it was not possible for Colville to take any other.
“And have you been able to divine from what you have seen here,” he asked gravely, “the grounds of Savonarola’s objection to the Carnival?”
“Not at all,” said the old man promptly. “I have seen nothing but the most harmless gaiety throughout the evening.”
Colville hung his head. He remembered reading once in a passage from Swedenborg, that the most celestial angels had scarcely any power of perceiving evil.
“Why aren’t you young people dancing?” asked Mr. Waters, in a cheerful general way, of Mrs. Bowen’s party.
Colville was glad to break the silence. “Mrs. Bowen doesn’t approve of dancing at vegliones.”
“No?﻿—why not?” inquired the old man, with invincible simplicity.
Mrs. Bowen smiled her pretty, small smile below her mask.
“The company is apt to be rather mixed,” she said quietly.
“Yes,” pursued Mr. Waters; “but you could dance with one another. The company seems very well behaved.”
“Oh, quite so,” Mrs. Bowen assented.
“Shortly after I came,” said Mr. Waters, “one of the masks asked me to dance. I was really sorry that my age and traditions forbade my doing so. I tried to explain, but I’m afraid I didn’t make myself quite clear.”
“Probably it passed for a joke with her,” said Colville, in order to say something.
“Ah, very likely; but I shall always feel that my impressions of the Carnival would have been more definite if could have danced. Now, if I were a young man like you﻿—”
Imogene turned and looked at Colville through the eye-holes of her mask; even in that sort of isolation he thought her eyes expressed surprise.
“It never occurred to you before that I was a young man,” he suggested gravely.
She did not reply.
After a little interval, “Imogene,” asked Mrs. Bowen, “would you like to dance?”
Colville was astonished. “The veglione has gone to your head, Mrs. Bowen,” he tacitly made his comment. She had spoken to Imogene, but she glanced at him as if she expected him to be grateful to her for this stroke of liberality.
“What would be the use?” returned the girl.
Colville rose. “After my performance in the Lancers, I can’t expect you to believe me; but I really *do* know how to waltz.” He had but to extend his arms, and she was hanging upon his shoulder, and they were whirling away through a long orbit of delight to the girl.
“Oh, why have you let me do you such injustice?” she murmured intensely. “I never shall forgive myself.”
“It grieved me that you shouldn’t have divined that I was really a magnificent dancer in disguise, but I bore it as best I could,” said Colville, really amused at her seriousness. “Perhaps you’ll find out after a while that I’m not an old fellow either, but only a ‘Lost Youth.’ ”
“Hush,” she said; “I don’t like to hear you talk so.”
“How?”
“About﻿—age!” she answered. “It makes me feel﻿—Don’t tonight!”
Colville laughed. “It isn’t a fact that my blinking is going to change materially. You had better make the most of me as a lost youth. I’m old enough to be two of them.”
She did not answer, and as they wound up and down through the other orbing couples, he remembered the veglione of seventeen years before, when he had dreamed through the waltz with the girl who jilted him; she was very docile and submissive that night; he believed afterward that if he had spoken frankly then, she would not have refused him. But he had veiled his passion in words and phrases that, taken in themselves, had no meaning﻿—that neither committed him nor claimed her. He could not help it; he had not the courage at any moment to risk the loss of her forever, till it was too late, till he must lose her.
“Do you believe in preexistence?” he demanded of Imogene.
“Oh yes!” she flashed back. “This very instant it was just as if I had been here before, long ago.”
“Dancing with me?”
“With you? Yes﻿—yes﻿—I think so.”
He had lived long enough to know that she was making herself believe what she said, and that she had not lived long enough to know this.
“Then you remember what I said to you﻿—tried to say to you﻿—that night?” Through one of those psychological juggles which we all practise with ourselves at times, it amused him, it charmed him, to find her striving to realise this past.
“No; it was so long ago? What was it?” she whispered dreamily.
A turn of the waltz brought them near Mrs. Bowen; her mask seemed to wear a dumb reproach. He began to be weary; one of the differences between youth and later life is that the latter wearies so soon of any given emotion.
“Ah, I can’t remember, either! Aren’t you getting rather tired of the waltz and me?”
“Oh no; go on!” she deeply murmured. “Try to remember.”
The long, pulsating stream of the music broke and fell. The dancers crookedly dispersed in wandering lines. She took his arm; he felt her heart leap against it; those innocent, trustful throbs upbraided him. At the same time his own heart beat with a sort of fond, protecting tenderness; he felt the witchery of his power to make this young, radiant, and beautiful creature hang flattered and bewildered on his talk; he liked the compassionate worship with which his tacit confidence had inspired her, even while he was not without some satirical sense of the crude sort of heartbroken hero he must be in the fancy of a girl of her age.
“Let us go and walk in the corridor a moment,” he said. But they walked there till the alluring melancholy music of the waltz began again. In a mutual caprice, they rejoined the dance.
It came into his head to ask, “Who is *he*?” and as he had got past denying himself anything, he asked it.
“He? What he?”
“He that Mrs. Bowen thought might object to your seeing the Carnival?”
“Oh!﻿—oh yes! That was the not impossible he.”
“Is that all?”
“Yes.”
“Then he’s not even the not improbable he?”
“No, indeed.”
They waltzed in silence. Then, “Why did you ask me that?” she murmured.
“I don’t know. Was it such a strange question?”
“I don’t know. You ought to.”
“Yes, if it was wrong, I’m old enough to know better.”
“You promised not to say ‘old’ any more.”
“Then I suppose I mustn’t. But you mustn’t get me to ignore it, and then laugh at me for it.”
“Oh!” she reproached him, “you think I could do that?”
“You could if it was you who were here with me once before.”
“Then I know I wasn’t.”
Again they were silent, and it was he who spoke first. “I wish you would tell me why you object to the interdicted topic?”
“Because﻿—because I like every time to be perfect in itself.”
“Oh! And this wouldn’t be perfect in itself if I were﻿—not so young as some people?”
“I didn’t mean that. No; but if you didn’t mention it, no one else would think of it or care for it.”
“Did anyone ever accuse you of flattering, Miss Graham?”
“Not till now. And you are unjust.”
“Well, I withdraw the accusation.”
“And will you ever pretend such a thing again?”
“Oh, never!”
“Then I have your promise.”
The talk was light wordplay, such as depends upon the talker’s own mood for its point or its pointlessness. Between two young people of equal years it might have had meanings to penetrate, to sigh over, to question. Colville found it delicious to be pursued by the ingenuous fervour of this young girl, eager to vindicate her sincerity in prohibiting him from his own ironical depreciation. Apparently, she had a sentimental mission of which he was the object; he was to be convinced that he was unnecessarily morbid; he was to be cheered up, to be kept in heart.
“I must believe in you after this,” he said, with a smile which his mask hid.
“Thanks,” she breathed. It seemed to him that her hand closed convulsively upon his in their light clasp.
The pressure sent a real pang to his heart. It forced her name from his lips. “Imogene! Ah, I’ve no right to call you that.”
“Yes.”
“From this out I promise to be twenty years younger. But no one is to know it but you. Do you think you will know it? I shouldn’t like to keep the secret to myself altogether.”
“No; I will help you. It shall be *our* secret.”
She gave a low laugh of delight. He convinced himself that she had entered into the light spirit of banter in which he believed that he was talking.
The music ceased again. He whirled her to the seat where he had left Mrs. Bowen. She was not there, nor the others.
Colville felt the meanness of a man who has betrayed his trust, and his self-contempt was the sharper because the trust had been as tacit and indefinite as it was generous. The effect of Mrs. Bowen’s absence was as if she had indignantly flown, and left him to the consequences of his treachery.
He sat down rather blankly with Imogene to wait for her return; it was the only thing they could do.
It had grown very hot. The air was thick with dust. The lights burned through it as through a fog.
“I believe I will take off my mask,” she said. “I can scarcely breathe.”
“No, no,” protested Colville; “that won’t do.”
“I feel faint,” she gasped.
His heart sank. “Don’t,” he said incoherently. “Come with me into the vestibule, and get a breath of air.”
He had almost to drag her through the crowd, but in the vestibule she revived, and they returned to their place again. He did not share the easy content with which she recognised the continued absence of Mrs. Bowen.
“Why they must be lost. But isn’t it perfect sitting here and watching the maskers?”
“Perfect,” said Colville distractedly.
“Don’t you like to make romances about the different ones?”
It was on Colville’s tongue to say that he had made all the romances he wished for that evening, but he only answered, “Oh, very.”
“Poor Mrs. Bowen,” laughed the girl. “It will be such a joke on her, with her punctilious notions, getting lost from her protégée at a Carnival ball! I shall tell everyone.”
“Oh no, don’t,” said Colville, in horror that his mask scarcely concealed.
“Why not?”
“It wouldn’t be at all the thing.”
“Why, are *you* becoming Europeanised too?” she demanded. “I thought you went in for all sorts of unconventionalities. Recollect your promise. You must be as impulsive as I am.”
Colville, staring anxiously about in every direction, made for the first time the reflection that most young girls probably conform to the proprieties without in the least knowing why.
“Do you think,” he asked, in desperation, “that you would be afraid to be left here a moment while I went about in the crowd and tried to find them?”
“Not at all,” she said. But she added, “Don’t be gone long.”
“Oh no,” he answered, pulling off his mask. “Be sure not to move from here on any account.”
He plunged into the midst of the crowd that buffeted him from side to side as he struck against its masses. The squeaking and gibbering masks mocked in their falsetto at his wild-eyed, naked face thrusting hither and thither among them.
“I saw your lady wife with another gentleman,” cried one of them, in a subtle misinterpretation of the cause of his distraction.
The throng had immensely increased; the clowns and harlequins ran shrieking up and down, and leaped over one another’s heads.
It was useless. He went back to Imogene with a heart-sickening fear that she too might have vanished.
But she was still there.
“You ought to have come sooner,” she said gaily. “That red mask has been here again. He looked as if he wanted to make love to *me* this time. But he didn’t. If you’d been here you might have asked him where Mrs. Bowen was.”
Colville sat down. He had done what he could to mend the matter, and the time had come for philosophical submission. It was now his duty to keep up Miss Graham’s spirits. They were both Americans, and from the national standpoint he was simply the young girl’s middle-aged bachelor friend. There was nothing in the situation for him to beat his breast about.
“Well, all that we can do is to wait for them,” he said.
“Oh yes,” she answered easily. “They’ll be sure to come back in the course of time.”
They waited a half-hour, talking somewhat at random, and still the others did not come. But the red mask came again. He approached Colville, and said politely﻿—
“*La signora è partita.*”
“The lady gone?” repeated Colville, taking this to be part of the red mask’s joke.
“*La bambina pareva poco lene.*”
“The little one not well?” echoed Colville again, rising. “Are you joking?”
The mask made a deep murmur of polite deprecation. “I am not capable of such a thing in a serious affair. Perhaps you know me?” he said, taking off his mask, and in further sign of good faith he gave the name of a painter sufficiently famous in Florence.
“I beg your pardon, and thank you,” said Colville. He had no need to speak to Imogene; her hand was already trembling on his arm.
They drove home in silence through the white moonlight of the streets, filled everywhere with the gay voices and figures of the Carnival.
Mrs. Bowen met them at the door of her apartment, and received them with a manner that justly distributed the responsibility and penalty for their escapade. Colville felt that a meaner spirit would have wreaked its displeasure upon the girl alone. She made short, quiet answers to all his eager inquiries. Most probably it was some childish indisposition; Effie had been faint. No, he need not go for the doctor. Mr. Waters had called the doctor, who had just gone away. There was nothing else that he could do for her. She dropped her eyes, and in everything but words dismissed him. She would not even remain with him till he could decently get himself out of the house. She left Imogene to receive his adieux, feigning that she heard Effie calling.
“I’m﻿—I’m very sorry,” faltered the girl, “that we didn’t go back to her at once.”
“Yes; I was to blame,” answered the humiliated hero of her Carnival dream. The clinging regret with which she kept his hand at parting scarcely consoled him for what had happened.
“I will come round in the morning,” he said. “I must know how Effie is.”
“Yes; come.”

===Chinese===

第二天早上，保罗端着科维尔的早餐上来，也带来了消息：佩尔戈拉剧院要办一场化装舞会（veglione）。这消息让科维尔提起了精神。"保罗，"他说，"你真该开一家银行。"保罗听惯了意大利语讲不利索的外国人跟他打趣；他微笑着，好像听懂了。
这位银行家对保罗的智力怀有精明的怀疑；在欧洲，银行家怀疑一切不是出自自家商号的新闻；可过了一两天，报上的广告把说服工作一直做到了银行家本人心里。
科维尔带着化装舞会的消息去找太太们，发现她们已经听说了。"你想去吗？"他问鲍文太太。
"我不知道。你看呢？"她反问。
"哦，动脑子的事归你。我只知道我想要什么。"
伊莫金不作声，只看着那场内心的辩论在鲍文太太脸上怎么一笔笔写出来。
"人们都坐包厢，"她若有所思地说，"可你会觉得，包厢到底不是那么回事，对吧？"
"咱们当年可是坐在场子里。"科维尔提了个醒。
"那时候情形大不一样。再说，芬利太太根本就一点儿体统观念也没有。"一个女人一旦把某一行动明明白白贬斥过了，换个条件稍微一变，她显然就攒足了照办的勇气。"当然，咱们要是下场子，我是一点儿也不愿意让人知道的——虽说外国人在这里差不多想干什么都行。"
"说真的，"科维尔说，"真到了那一步，我看不出这有什么不好。"
"那么你说去？"
"你怎么说，我就怎么说。"
鲍文太太看看他，又看看伊莫金。"我也看不出。"她末了说。两人都明白，她指的正是他没看出来的那个"不好"。
"咱们谁修来这么大的福分？"科维尔问。
"哦，你们全都修到了。"她说，"咱们戴面具、穿化装斗篷去。什么也不会发生；就算发生了，谁认得咱们？"她们手里本来有鲍尔盖塞大舞会的请帖——那至今还是佛罗伦萨嘉年华里外国人眼中时髦而抢手的大事；可她们对化装舞会的种种先入之见，把佛罗伦萨绅士们献给受宠的侨客的那份招待都比下去了。
"来。"鲍文太太说，"你得跟我们去，帮我们挑化装斗篷。"
一个谨慎的女人做起不谨慎的事来，从不做半截。埃菲也获准同去化装舞会，她跟着他们到了租斗篷的铺子。鲍文太太说，在铺子里自己挑，比叫人送到家里看有趣得多。埃菲穿黑的；伊莫金挑一件浅蓝斗篷；鲍文太太选了紫的；连脸都不打算露的地方，她们挑颜色也要挑着肤色来。万一撞见熟人，想摘下面具，总不能显得吓人。铺子里的人对这一切表现出最鲜活的兴趣，好像这对他们也是新鲜事，这还是他们头一回伺候租面具斗篷的外国客。他们让鲍文太太和伊莫金进里间换了出来，专叫科维尔纳闷——他套着面具斗篷在前店晃来晃去，一副大块头。
"哪个是哪个？"两位太太一出来就用面具人惯用的假嗓子齐声考他。
他拿出男人的严整逻辑，按绸子颜色分辨；可她们早想到换过来了，摘下面具，冲着他的脸大笑。
她们在那一挂一挂的面具和斗篷中间轻飘飘地转来转去，抖落下一股旧嘉年华幽灵似的香气，他的心一跳。
"啊，你们再也不会这么迷人了！"他喊。他真想把她们俩都搂进怀里——两个人都这么可爱；男人表达他对女人的至高满意，至今还只有这一条原始的路子。"喏，我是哪个？"他反过来问她们，又惹得她们笑。他当真已经把胳膊搂上了埃菲。
"你觉得化装舞会上你认得出你爸爸吗？"铺子里一个女人问，对这家子和气的一行人兴趣越来越浓。
大家都笑了；这个自然的误会，伊莫金觉得尤其滑稽。
"来吧，"鲍文太太喊，"该走了。"
也真是；她们在铺子里泡得太久，再一本正经地杀价都理不直气不壮了。头一个价就认了。那女人说得也在理：嘉年华呢，东西她要多少是多少。
十一点，她们去赴化装舞会，照旧由鲍文太太的马车来接科维尔。他戴着面具、披着斗篷走出房间，觉得怪臊的；好在旅馆走廊里空空的，多半还黑着；只有门房没睡，祝他玩得痛快，那口气平淡得像他穿着燕尾服出门赴个平常晚会。马车里，太太们把冒险的气氛随身散布，他的兴头也越坐越高；他一上车，埃菲·鲍文就孩子气地放声笑了。
窄街上，来来往往的马车车轮轰响；剧场门前那条街挤得水泄不通，好一阵子才挨到门口。假面人进进出出；太太们那份紧张的欢喜，化作一声深深的、发颤的叹息。车门由剧场的一个仆役拉开，祝她们化装舞会愉快；下一刻，她们已经站在拥挤的门厅里，停了一小会儿，好让伊莫金和埃菲切切实实感到：自己是这场化装盛会的一部分了。
"都跟紧了。"鲍文太太说。她们穿过门厅的内门，灯火辉煌的剧场把它的一片色彩和浮华泼到她们脸上。正厅的地板已经填得同台面齐平，舞台卸去了布景，向那群花花绿绿的人马敞开更空阔的场地——他们正旋着华尔兹在上面打转。包厢一层叠一层，被枝形烛台照得通明，烛光同煤气灯焰交相争辉。
"你和埃菲走前头。"鲍文太太对伊莫金说。她叫她们俩像孩子一样手拉手，自己则不由自主地把手挽进了科维尔的胳膊。
一个从头到脚一身红的假面人贴上了这一伙，用一流的哑剧向她求起爱来。
科维尔着恼了。他问她要不要他去叫那家伙滚开。
"千万别！"她答，"这好玩极了。没有这一手就不算化装舞会。你见过这么好的演技吗？"
"我看除了热情，没什么了不起。"科维尔说。
"我倒想看看你向哪位太太求爱是什么样。"她促狭地回敬。
"你要愿意，我就向你求。"他说，可话一出口就觉得这玩笑失了分寸。
他们把剧场兜了一圈。"那是斯特罗齐亲王，伊莫金。"鲍文太太俯身对姑娘咬耳朵。她又指认出包厢里其他一些有历史、有门第的姓氏；包厢里的太太们自成一种美的民主，个个涂脂抹粉到同一种"侯爵夫人"的效果。
场子上是穿晚礼服不戴面具的先生们，间或有几位跳舞的太太，戴面具而不披斗篷。可大部分人还是穿行头；剧场在舞里流过的一派斑斓色调里红涨、开花，晃得人眼花。
鲍文太太早已把这场冒险圈好了界，跳舞圈在外面。伊莫金不许跳。坐包厢看，可以；再往前一步下了场子，那就断乎不许跳。
这一点事前说得透透的，到了剧场也不许请愿、不许嘀咕。他们找个僻静角落坐下观望。
红衣假面人跟了过来，在稍远处占了位子；鲍文太太的目光转到哪边，他就把他的热情再表白一遍。
"你肯定他不烦你？"科维尔提醒。
"不，一点不。他逗极了。"
"哦，那就好！"
华尔兹停了；那团旋转缠绕的乱流散作一股股歪歪扭扭的人溪，来来去去，上上下下。他们开始挑拣起叫他们感兴趣的衣服和角色来。白衣小丑顶着大鼻子，杂色斑衣的丑角戴着扁平黑面具，满场都是。有几只绿色的蚱蜢妙极了，额头上颤着长长的触须。两三个梅菲斯特在人堆里透出红来。几位甲胄骑士走得艰难，看样子叫护胫和胸甲压坏了。
一伙蹦蹦跳跳的假面人围住一个穿晚礼服、长头发的年轻人——他倚在近旁一根柱子上，含着半好笑半受刑的笑容，受着他们的嘲弄。
他们作弄他作弄够了、正四下张望别的猎物时，红假面人对鲍文太太的殷勤加倍地卖弄起来，别的假面人围拢来喝彩。
"哦，这回，"她说，轻轻笑了，笑声里没有一点不快，倒有点窘，"我想你可以请他走开了。不过别对他凶。"她见科维尔朝假面人迈出一副要动粗的步子，又补了一句。
"哦，我对他凶什么？咱们又不是情敌。"这话同样不得体，科维尔自觉。"再说，我也是意大利人。"他找补了一句。他朝假面人踱了几步，低声婉转地说："太太觉得有点不方便。"
假面人一下栽进一个滑稽绝望的姿势里，手按心口深深一躬，以示降服，随即消失在人海里。其余的喝着彩散了。
"你们俩办得多漂亮！"鲍文太太说，"我开始相信您真是意大利人了，科维尔先生。我要怕您了。"
"您可不怕他。"
"哦，他是真意大利人。"
"我看妈妈一个人把化装舞会的好处全占了。"埃菲幽幽地抱怨。这个家教顶好的孩子，一定是憋屈到深处，才发出这句犯上的声音。
"可不是嘛，埃菲，"她妈妈答，"我自己也觉得不公道。怎么办呢？"
"我想吃点东西。"孩子说。
"我也想。"科维尔说，"这是你妈妈欠咱们全体的赔礼。叫她带咱们去找吃的。格雷厄姆小姐，来份冰淇淋好不好？"
"好，一份冰淇淋。"伊莫金说，那口气像在说"别的打死也不要"，科维尔笑了。她梦游似的慢慢站起来，隔着面具朝她抛下的场子投去一眼——一眼能有多热，那一眼就有多热。乐队又奏起华尔兹，宽阔的地板上满是打转的一对对。
摆桌子那条走廊挤满了人，喝啤酒的，吃冷牛肉、去骨火鸡和大圆香肠切片的。"哦，他们怎么吃得下！"姑娘打了个寒喳叫道。
"我还不知道你对这种事这么不食人间烟火，"科维尔说，"我以为乌切利太太家那份沙拉你并不反对。"
"哦，可这是化装舞会呀！"姑娘轻轻吐出这一句，算是全部回答。他又笑了；鲍文太太却没跟着笑；他纳闷为什么。
回到剧场里她们的角落，他们发现那儿立着一位黑斗篷假面人，让了座，仍站在近旁。他们无拘无束、敞着嗓门聊起天来——说英语的人在意大利就是这个改不了的脾气，他们的话在那里差不多算得上本国语言。
"说真的，"科维尔说，"戴这面具我怕要憋死。太太们要是肯想法子围住我、替我保密，我就摘下来一会儿。"
"那我跟您一起摘，科维尔先生。"近旁那位假面人说。他推起那片小小的丝面罩，露出沃特斯先生那张温厚慈祥的脸。
"我的天！"科维尔叫出声。
鲍文太太像是惊得说不出话。
"没想到在这儿碰见我？"老人问，口气仿佛不然的话，这是天底下最自然的事。事已如此，大家只好齐心把惊讶压下去。"有意思极了，"他接着说，"有意思极了！节目一开场我就在这儿，不但大大地取了乐，还大大地受了教。依我看，这是理解这个奇妙民族的历史上许多怪事的一把钥匙。"
沃特斯先生既对嘉年华拿出了这个哲学态度，科维尔也就只能拿出同样的态度。
"那么，从这儿看到的，"他严肃地问，"您能参悟出萨伏那洛拉反对嘉年华的缘由吗？"
"完全参悟不出。"老人干脆地说，"一晚上我只看见了最无害的欢乐。"
科维尔低下头。他记起从前在斯威登堡的一段文字里读到：最高层的天使几乎感觉不到恶。
"你们年轻人怎么不跳舞？"沃特斯先生冲着鲍文太太这一伙，快快活活地问了一句。
科维尔巴不得打破沉默。"鲍文太太不赞成在化装舞会上跳舞。"
"不赞成？——为什么？"老人问，一副打不垮的天真。
鲍文太太在面具底下露出她那秀气的小小一笑。
"这儿的人杂。"她安静地说。
"是啊，"沃特斯先生接着说，"可你们可以彼此跳嘛。这些人看着挺规矩的。"
"哦，可不。"鲍文太太应承。
"我刚来不大会儿，"沃特斯先生说，"有个假面人请我跳舞。我的年纪和我的老规矩不许我跳，真叫我遗憾。我试着解释，只怕没讲清楚。"
"在她听来八成是句玩笑。"科维尔说，为了说点什么。
"啊，很可能；不过我永远会觉得，要是能跳，我对嘉年华的印象就更完整了。我要是像您这样的年轻人——"
伊莫金转过脸，从面具的眼孔里望着科维尔；连隔着那一层隔绝，他都觉出她眼里写满了惊讶。
"您以前从没想过我是个年轻人。"他郑重地提了一句。
她没答话。
隔了一小会儿，"伊莫金，"鲍文太太问，"你想跳舞吗？"
科维尔吃了一惊。"化装舞会上了您的头了，鲍文太太。"他心里默默评了一句。她问的是伊莫金，眼睛却瞟着他，好像指望他为这一手宽大感激她。
"跳有什么用？"姑娘回了一句。
科维尔站起身。"有我在兰切斯舞里那番表演在前，您没法信我；可我确实会跳华尔兹。"他只消张开双臂，她已挂上他的肩，两人旋了出去，绕着那姑娘转出一条长长的、心醉神迷的轨道。
"哦，您为什么让我那样冤枉您？"她热烈地喃喃道，"我永远不能原谅自己。"
"您竟没猜出我其实是一位化了装的绝世舞家，我很伤心；可我尽力忍了。"科维尔说，她这份认真真叫他好笑。"兴许过一阵您还会发现，我也根本不是个老头儿，不过是个'迷途的青年'。"
"嘘，"她说，"我不爱听您这么讲话。"
"怎么讲？"
"讲——年纪！"她答，"那叫我觉得——今晚别讲！"
科维尔笑了。"我眨眼也变不成年轻人，这是事实。您最好趁我还是个迷途青年，把我用足。我的岁数够当两个。"
她不答。两人绕着别的转圈的伴侣上上下下地盘旋，他想起十七年前那场化装舞会，他同后来甩了他的那个姑娘一路梦着跳完华尔兹；那一夜她温顺极了；他事后一直相信，要是他当时把话说透，她是不会拒绝他的。可他偏把他的感情裹在一层自身全无意义的话和词句里——既不把自己押上去，也不向她索要什么。他没法不这样；他始终没有那份勇气，在任何一个时刻冒永远失去她的险——直到太迟了，直到他非失去她不可了。
"你相信前世吗？"他问伊莫金。
"哦，信！"她闪回来，"就在这一瞬间，我真像很久很久以前来过这儿。"
"同我跳过舞？"
"同你？对——对——我想是。"
他活到了会看出的岁数：她是在让自己相信她的话；而她还没活到能自知这一点的岁数。
"那你记得我那晚对你说的——想对你说的话？"我们人人时不时都对自己玩那一套心理戏法；看她使劲去够那个过去，他觉得好玩，又觉得迷人心魄。
"不记得了；太久远了吧？是什么？"她梦呓似的低声问。
华尔兹一个转身把他们带到鲍文太太近旁；她的面具像挂着一副无言的责备。他开始累了；青春与后来的岁月的一大差别，就是后者对随便哪一种情绪，都腻得那么快。
"啊，我也不记得了！华尔兹加我，你是不是有点腻了？"
"哦，不；接着跳！"她低低地喃喃，"你想想看。"
音乐那长长的、搏动的流水断了、落了。跳舞的人七歪八扭散成游动的线。她挽住他的胳膊；他觉出她的心撞在臂上；那一下下天真、信赖的跳动，句句数落他。同时，他自己的心也跳着一种疼惜的、护佑的温情；他觉出自己那份魔力的迷人：能叫这个年轻、焕发、美丽的生灵，被他的谈吐捧得又晕又醉地挂着；他喜欢他无言的自信在她心里唤起的那种近乎崇拜的怜惜——尽管他也不是没有几分讥请地想到，在她那个年纪的姑娘想象里，他这副"心碎英雄"该多么粗制滥造。
"咱们去走廊走一会儿吧。"他说。可他们在走廊走到那支勾人惆怅的华尔兹又响起，两人一齐心痒，重新入舞。
一个念头跳进他脑子："'他'是谁？"他已经到了不肯亏待自己的地步，就问了。
"他？哪个他？"
"鲍文太太认为也许会反对你看嘉年华的那个他呀。"
"哦！——哦，对！那是那个'并非不可能的他'。"
"就这些？"
"就这些。"
"那他甚至连那个'并非全无指望的他'都算不上？"
"不算，差得远。"
两人默默跳着。过了一阵，"你为什么问我这个？"她喃喃。
"不知道。这问题很怪吗？"
"不知道。你该知道。"
"是；要是问错了，我这岁数也该懂事点了。"
"你答应了再也不说'老'字。"
"那我就不说。可你也不能先哄我忘了它，回头又拿它笑我。"
"哦！"她嗔他，"你以为我会那样？"
"要是从前往这儿跟我跳过舞的是你，你就会。"
"那我可知道：从前的不是我。"
两人又不说话了，还是他先开的口："你能告诉我，你为什么忌讳那个犯禁的题目吗？"
"因为——因为我喜欢每一回都完完整整、自成一体。"
"哦！那要是我不像有些人那么年轻，这一回就不完整了？"
"我不是那个意思。不；你要是不提，别人就不会想到它、在乎它。"
"有人说过你嘴甜吗，格雷厄姆小姐？"
"从前没有。你这冤枉人了。"
"好，我收回。"
"你以后还装这样的冤枉吗？"
"哦，再也不了！"
"那这就算你答应了。"
这场谈话全是轻飘飘的言语游戏，有意思没意思，全看说话人自己那一刻的心境。放在两个同龄的年轻人中间，这些话底下可以有钻的、叹的、追问的意思。科维尔被这年轻姑娘的一腔赤诚追着，只觉甜美——她急赤白脸地要证明自己的真心，不许他自嘲自贬。看样子，她把他当成了多愁善感的使命的对象；她要叫他相信，他的忧郁毫无必要；要给他打气，要他撑住心气。
"这以后我不得不信你了。"他说，笑容叫面具挡着。
"谢谢。"她轻轻吐出。他觉得，两人轻轻相扣的手里，她那只突然痉挛地攥紧了。
这一握，真扎了他心口一下。它把她的名字从他嘴上挤了出来。"伊莫金！啊，我没权利这样叫你。"
"有。"
"从今往后，我答应年轻二十岁。可这事除了你谁也不许知道。你想你认得出吗？这秘密我可不乐意一个人独吞。"
"认得出；我帮你。这就成了咱们的秘密。"
她发出一声低低的、欢喜的笑。他说服了自己：他相信自己是拿打趣的轻巧腔在讲话，而她也进到了这个腔里。
音乐又停了。他旋着她回到他撇下鲍文太太的座位。她不在，别人也不在。
科维尔体味到一个辜负了所托的男人的卑鄙；他的自责格外锋利，因为那托付慷慨，却又默契而无形。鲍文太太这一不在，效果就像她愤然而去，把他丢给他背信的下场。
他同伊莫金发着怔坐下等她回来；也只有这一条路。
场子里热起来了。空气里全是尘土。灯火隔着它烧，像隔着雾。
"我要摘面具了，"她说，"憋得喘不上气。"
"别，别。"科维尔拦她，"那不行。"
"我发晕。"她喘着。
他的心往下一沉。"别——"他语无伦次地说，"跟我到门厅去，透口气。"
他几乎是把她从人堆里拖出来的；到了门厅她缓过来，两人又回到原地。她看着鲍文太太还是不在，那份安然，他可没有。
"他们准是走丢了。可坐在这儿看着满场假面人，不是再好没有吗？"
"再好没有。"科维尔心不在焉地说。
"你不爱给他们一个个编故事吗？"
科维尔嘴边正有一句：今晚的故事我已经编够了；他只答了句："哦，爱编。"
"可怜的鲍文太太，"姑娘笑起来，"她那么讲究规矩，倒在一个嘉年华舞会上把自己被监护人丢了！这笑话够她受的。我要讲给所有人听。"
"哦，别。"科维尔说，那份惊慌，面具几乎遮不住。
"为什么别？"
"那可太不像话了。"
"怎么，你也变欧化了？"她质问，"我还当你什么都敢不拘呢。记着你的承诺。你必须跟我一样冲动。"
科维尔急得四下张望，头一回悟到一条道理：大多数年轻姑娘守着那些规矩，其实全不知其所以然。
"你看，"他绝望地问，"我把你一个人留在这儿一小会儿，我去各处找找他们，你怕不怕？"
"一点也不。"她说。可她又添了一句，"别去太久。"
"不会。"他答着，扯下了面具，"你千万别离开这儿，无论如何。"
他一头扎进人海，人浪从两边推搡着他。尖声怪气的假面人们用假嗓子嘲笑他这张横冲直撞、两眼发直的裸脸。
"我看见你太太跟另一位先生走了。"其中一个喊，把他方寸大乱的原因微妙地猜岔了。
人潮涨了许多；小丑和丑角尖叫着跑上跑下，从彼此头上翻过去。
没指望了。他回到伊莫金身边，心口发凉地怕着：她兴许也不见了。
可她还在。
"你该早点回来。"她欢欢喜喜地说，"那个红假面人又来过了。看那意思，这回是想向我求爱。可他没求。你要在，倒可以问问他鲍文太太在哪儿。"
科维尔坐下。能做的他都做了，如今到了哲学地认命的时辰。他眼下的本分是给格雷厄姆小姐提气。他们俩都是美国人；站在国人的立场上，他不过是这位年轻姑娘的中年单身朋友。这局面里没有任何一件事够他捶胸顿足。
"好吧，咱们只有等了。"他说。
"哦，是。"她轻轻松松地应，"他们早晚准回来。"
他们等了半个钟头，有一搭没一搭地聊着，那几位还是不来。红假面人倒又来了。他凑近科维尔，客客气气地说：
"La signora è partita.（太太走了。）"
"太太走了？"科维尔只当这是红假面人玩笑的一部分，跟着重复。
"La bambina pareva poco lene.（小姑娘好像不太舒服。）"
"小姑娘不舒服？"科维尔又应了一声，站了起来，"你开玩笑？"
假面人低低地、客气地哼了一声不敢当。"正经事上我开不出这种玩笑。也许您认得我？"他说着摘下面具，为进一步取信，又报出一个在佛罗伦萨够有名气的画家的名字。
"失敬，谢谢。"科维尔说。他不用对伊莫金讲；她的手已经在他胳膊上发抖了。
他们乘车回家，一路无言，穿过街上一片白蒙蒙的月光，满街到处是嘉年华欢快的声与影。
鲍文太太在寓所门口迎着他们，用一种把这次出格的责任与罪罚分摊得恰到好处的态度接待了他们。科维尔觉出，一个气量小些的人，会把不痛快全冲着姑娘撒。对他急切的询问，她只做了简短安静的回答。多半是孩子家的一点小不舒服；埃菲晕了一下。不，他不必去请大夫。沃特斯先生已经请过了，大夫刚走。再没有别的可效劳的了。她垂下眼，除了言语之外的一切都在送客。她甚至不肯陪他挨到一个得体的告辞时刻，托词听见埃菲叫她，把伊莫金留下受他的道别。
"我——我很抱歉，"姑娘嗫嚅道，"咱们没有马上回到她身边去。"
"是；怪我。"她的嘉年华之梦那位丢了脸的英雄答。分手时她恋恋不舍地攫着他的手，那点温存，几乎安慰不了他所遭遇的一切。
"我明天上午过来。"他说，"我得知道埃菲怎么样了。"
"好；来吧。"

## V / 五
===Original===

Colville fell asleep with the flattered sense which abounds in the heart of a young man after his first successful evening in society, but which can visit maturer life only upon some such conditions of long exile and return as had been realised in his. The looks of these two charming women followed him into his dreams; he knew he must have pleased them, the dramatic homage of the child was evidence of that; and though it had been many years since he had found it sufficient cause of happiness to have pleased a woman, the desire to do so was by no means extinct in him. The eyes of the girl hovered above him like stars; he felt in their soft gaze that he was a romance to her young heart, and this made him laugh; it also made him sigh.
He woke at dawn with a sharp twinge in his shoulder, and he rose to give himself the pleasure of making his own fire with those fagots of broom and pine twigs which he had enjoyed the night before, promising himself to get back into bed when the fire was well going, and sleep late. While he stood before the open stove, the jangling of a small bell outside called him to the window, and he saw a procession which had just issued from the church going to administer the extreme unction to some dying person across the piazza. The parish priest went first, bearing the consecrated wafer in its vessel, and at his side an acolyte holding a yellow silk umbrella over the Eucharist; after them came a number of *facchini* in white robes and white hoods that hid their faces; their tapers burned sallow and lifeless in the new morning light; the bell jangled dismally.
“They even die dramatically in this country,” thought Colville, in whom the artist was taken with the effectiveness of the spectacle before his human pity was stirred for the poor soul who was passing. He reproached himself for that, and instead of getting back to bed, he dressed and waited for the mature hour which he had ordered his breakfast for. When it came at last, picturesquely borne on the open hand of Giovanni, steaming coffee, hot milk, sweet butter in delicate disks, and two white eggs coyly tucked in the fold of a napkin, and all grouped upon the wide salver, it brought him a measure of the consolation which good cheer imparts to the ridiculous human heart even in the house where death is. But the sad incident tempered his mind with a sort of pensiveness that lasted throughout the morning, and quite till lunch. He spent the time in going about the churches; but the sunshine which the day began with was overcast, as it was the day before, and the churches were rather too dark and cold in the afternoon. He went to Viesseux’s reading-room and looked over the English papers, which he did not care for much; and he also made a diligent search of the catalogue for some book about Florence for little Effie Bowen: he thought he would like to surprise her mother with his interest in the matter. As the day waned toward dark, he felt more and more tempted to take her at her word, when she had said that any day was her day to him, and go to see her. If he had been a younger man he would have anxiously considered this indulgence and denied himself, but after forty a man denies himself no reasonable and harmless indulgence; he has learned by that time that it is a pity and a folly to do so.
Colville found Mrs. Bowen’s room half full of arriving and departing visitors, and then he remembered that it was this day she had named to him on the Ponte Vecchio, and when Miss Graham thanked him for coming his first Thursday, he made a merit of not having forgotten it, and said he was going to come every Thursday during the winter. Miss Graham drew him a cup of tea from the Russian samovar which replaces in some Florentine houses the teapot of Occidental civilisation, and Colville smiled upon it and upon her, bending over the brazen urn with a flowerlike tilt of her beautiful head. She wore an aesthetic dress of creamy camel’s-hair, whose colour pleased the eye as its softness would have flattered the touch.
“What a very Turgenevish effect the samovar gives!” he said, taking a biscuit from the basket Effie Bowen brought him, shrinking with redoubled shyness from the eyebrows which he arched at her. “I wonder you can keep from calling me Fedor Colvillitch. Where is your mother, Effie Bowenovna?” he asked of the child, with a temptation to say Imogene Grahamovna.
They both looked mystified, but Miss Graham said, “I’m sorry to say you won’t see Mrs. Bowen today. She has a very bad headache, and has left Effie and me to receive. We feel very incompetent, but she says it will do us good.”
There were some people there of the night before, and Colville had to talk to them. One of the ladies asked him if he had met the Inglehart boys as he came in.
“The Inglehart boys? No. What are the Inglehart boys?”
“They were here all last winter, and they’ve just got back. It’s rather exciting for Florence.” She gave him a rapid sketch of that interesting exodus of a score of young painters from the art school at Munich, under the head of the singular and fascinating genius by whose name they became known. “They had their own school for a while in Munich, and then they all came down into Italy in a body. They had their studio things with them, and they travelled third class, and they made the greatest excitement everywhere, and had the greatest fun. They were a great sensation in Florence. They went everywhere, and were such favourites. I hope they are going to stay.”
“I hope so too,” said Colville. “I should like to see them.”
“Dear me!” said the lady, with a glance at the clock. “It’s five! I must be going.”
The other ladies went, and Colville approached to take leave, but Miss Graham detained him.
“What is Turgenevish?” she demanded.
“The quality of the great Russian novelist, Turgenev,” said Colville, perceiving that she had not heard of him.
“Oh!”
“You ought to read him. The samovar sends up its agreeable odour all through his books. Read *Lisa* if you want your heart really broken.
“I’m glad you approve of heartbreaks in books. So many people won’t read anything but cheerful books. It’s the only quarrel I have with Mrs. Bowen. She says there are so many sad things in life that they ought to be kept out of books.”
“Ah, there I perceive a divided duty,” said Colville. “I should like to agree with both of you. But if Mrs. Bowen were here I should remind her that if there are so many sad things in life that is a very good reason for putting them in books too.”
“Of course I shall tell her what you said.”
“Why, I don’t object to a certain degree of cheerfulness in books; only don’t carry it too far﻿—that’s all.”
This made the young girl laugh, and Colville was encouraged to go on. He told her of the sight he had seen from his window at daybreak, and he depicted it all very graphically, and made her feel its pathos perhaps more keenly than he had felt it. “Now, that little incident kept with me all day, tempering my boisterous joy in the Giottos, and reducing me to a decent composure in the presence of the Cimabues; and it’s pretty hard to keep from laughing at some of them, don’t you think?”
The young people perceived that he was making fun again; but he continued with an air of greater seriousness. “Don’t you see what a very good thing that was to begin one’s day with? Why, even in Santa Croce, with the thermometer ten degrees below zero in the shade of Alfieri’s monument, I was no gayer than I should have been in a church at home. I suppose Mrs. Bowen would object to having that procession go by under one’s window in a book; but I can’t really see how it would hurt the reader, or damp his spirits permanently. A wholesome reaction would ensue, such as you see now in me, whom the thing happened to in real life.”
He stirred his tea, and shook with an inward laugh as he carried it to his lips.
“Yes,” said Miss Graham thoughtfully, and she looked at him searchingly in the interval of silence that ensued. But she only added, “I wish it would get warmer in the churches. I’ve seen hardly anything of them yet.”
“From the way I felt in them today,” sighed Colville, “I should think the churches would begin to thaw out about the middle of May. But if one goes well wrapped up in furs, and has a friend along to rouse him and keep him walking when he is about to fall into that lethargy which precedes death by freezing, I think they may be visited even now with safety. Have you been in Santa Maria Novella yet?”
“No,” said Miss Graham, with a shake of the head that expressed her resolution to speak the whole truth if she died for it, “not even in Santa Maria Novella.”
“What a wonderful old place it is! That curious façade, with the dials and its layers of black and white marble soaked golden-red in a hundred thousand sunsets! That exquisite grand portal!” He gesticulated with the hand that the teacup left free, to suggest form and measurement as artists do. “Then the inside! The great Cimabue, with all that famous history on its back﻿—the first divine Madonna by the first divine master, carried through the streets in a triumph of art and religion! Those frescoes of Ghirlandajo’s with real Florentine faces and figures in them, and all lavished upon the eternal twilight of that choir﻿—but I suppose that if the full day were let in on them, once, they would vanish like ghosts at cockcrow! You must be sure to see the Spanish chapel; and the old cloister itself is such a pathetic place. There’s a boys’ school, as well as a military college, in the suppressed convent now, and the colonnades were full of boys running and screaming and laughing and making a joyful racket; it was so much more sorrowful than silence would have been there. One of the little scamps came up to me, and the young monk that was showing me round, and bobbed us a mocking bow and bobbed his hat off; then they all burst out laughing again and raced away, and the monk looked after them and said, so sweetly and wearily, ‘They’re at their diversions: we must have patience.’ There are only twelve monks left there; all the rest are scattered and gone.” He gave his cup to Miss Graham for more tea.
“Don’t you think,” she asked, drawing it from the samovar, “that it is very sad having the convents suppressed?”
“It was very sad having slavery abolished﻿—for some people,” suggested Colville; he felt the unfairness of the point he had made.
“Yes,” sighed Miss Graham.
Colville stood stirring his second cup of tea, when the portiere parted, and showed Mrs. Bowen wistfully pausing on the threshold. Her face was pale, but she looked extremely pretty there.
“Ah, come in, Mrs. Bowen!” he called gaily to her. “I won’t give you away to the other people. A cup of tea will do you good.”
“Oh, I’m a great deal better,” said Mrs. Bowen, coming forward to give him her hand. “I heard your voice, and I couldn’t resist looking in.”
“That was very kind of you,” said Colville gratefully: and her eyes met his in a glance that flushed her face a deep red. “You find me here﻿—*I* don’t know why!﻿—in my character of old family friend, doing my best to make life a burden to the young ladies.”
“I wish you would stay to a family dinner with us,” said Mrs. Bowen, and Miss Graham brightened in cordial support of the hospitality. “Why can’t you?”
“I don’t know, unless it’s because I’m a humane person, and have some consideration for your headache.”
“Oh, that’s all gone,” said Mrs. Bowen. “It was one of those convenient headaches﻿—if you ever had them, you’d know﻿—that go off at sunset.”
“But you’d have another tomorrow.”
“No, I’m safe for a whole fortnight from another.”
“Then you leave me without an excuse, and I was just wishing I had none,” said Colville.
After dinner Mrs. Bowen sent Effie to bed early to make up for the late hours of the night before, but she sat before the fire with Miss Graham rather late, talking Colville over, when he was gone.
“He’s very puzzling to me,” said Miss Graham. “Sometimes you think he’s nothing but an old cynic, from his talk, and then something so sweet and fresh comes out that you don’t know what to do. Don’t you think he has really a very poetical mind, and that he’s putting all the rest on?”
“I think he likes to make little effects,” said Mrs. Bowen judiciously. “He always did, rather.”
“Why, was he like this when he was young?”
“I don’t consider him very old now.”
“No, of course not. I meant when you knew him before.” Miss Graham had some needlework in her hand; Mrs. Bowen, who never even pretended to work at that kind of thing, had nothing in hers but the feather screen.
“He is old, compared with you, Imogene; but you’ll find, as you live along, that your contemporaries are always young. Mr. Colville is very much improved. He used to be painfully shy, but he put on a bold front, and now the bold front seems to have become a second nature with him.”
“I like it,” said Miss Graham, to her needle.
“Yes; but I suspect he’s still shy, at heart. He used to be very sentimental, and was always talking Ruskin. I think if he hadn’t talked Ruskin so much, Jenny Milbury might have treated him better. It was very priggish in him.”
“Oh, I can’t imagine Mr. Colville’s being priggish!”
“He’s very much improved. He used to be quite a sloven in his dress; you know how very slovenly most American gentlemen are in their dress, at any rate. I think that influenced her against him too.”
“He isn’t slovenly now,” suggested Miss Graham.
“Oh no; he’s quite swell,” said Mrs. Bowen, depriving the adjective of slanginess by the refinement of her tone.
“Well,” said Miss Graham, “I don’t see how you could have endured her after that. It was atrocious.”
“It was better for her to break with him, if she found out she didn’t love him, than to marry him. That,” said Mrs. Bowen, with a depth of feeling uncommon for her, “would have been a thousand times worse.”
“Yes, but she ought to have found out before she led him on so far.”
“Sometimes girls can’t. They don’t know themselves; they think they’re in love when they’re not. She was very impulsive, and of course she was flattered by it; he was so intellectual. But at last she found that she couldn’t bear it, and she had to tell him so.”
“Did she ever say why she didn’t love him?”
“No; I don’t suppose she could. The only thing I remember her saying was that he was ‘too much of a mixture.’ ”
“What *did* she mean by that?”
“I don’t know exactly.”
“Do you think he’s insincere?”
“Oh no. Perhaps she meant that he wasn’t single-minded.”
“Fickle?”
“No. He certainly wasn’t that in her case.”
“Undecided?”
“He was decided enough with her﻿—at last.”
Imogene dropped the hopeless quest, “How can a man ever stand such a thing?” she sighed.
“He stood it very nobly. That was the best thing about it; he took it in the most delicate way. She showed me his letter. There wasn’t a word or a hint of reproach in it; he seemed to be anxious about nothing but her feeling badly for him. Of course he couldn’t help showing that he was mortified for having pursued her with attentions that were disagreeable to her; but that was delicate too. Yes, it was a very large-minded letter.”
“It was shocking in her to show it.”
“It wasn’t very nice. But it was a letter that any girl might have been proud to show.”
“Oh, she *couldn’t* have done it to gratify her vanity!”
“Girls are very queer, my dear,” said Mrs. Bowen, as if the fact were an abstraction. She mused upon the flat of her screen, while Miss Graham plied her needle in silence.
The latter spoke first. “Do you think he was very much broken by it?”
“You never can tell. He went out west then, and there he has stayed ever since. I suppose his life would have been very different if nothing of the kind had happened. He had a great deal of talent. I always thought I should hear of him in some way.”
“Well, it was a heartless, shameless thing! I don’t see how you can speak of it so leniently as you do, Mrs. Bowen. It makes all sorts of coquetry and flirtation more detestable to me than ever. Why, it has ruined his life!”
“Oh, he was young enough then to outlive it. After all, they were a boy and girl.”
“A boy and girl! How old were they?”
“He must have been twenty-three or four, and she was twenty.”
“*My* age! Do you call that being a girl?”
“She was old enough to know what she was about,” said Mrs. Bowen justly.
Imogene fell back in her chair, drawing out her needle the full length of its thread, and then letting her hand fall. “I don’t know. It seems as if I never should be grown up, or anything but a child. Yes, when I think of the way young men talk, they do seem boys. Why can’t they talk like Mr. Colville? I wish I could talk like him. It makes you forget how old and plain he is.”
She remained with her eyelids dropped in an absent survey of her sewing, while Mrs. Bowen regarded her with a look of vexation, impatience, resentment, on the last refinement of these emotions, which she banished from her face before Miss Graham looked up and said, with a smile: “How funny it is to see Effie’s infatuation with him! She can’t take her eyes off him for a moment, and she follows him round the room so as not to lose a word he is saying. It was heroic of her to go to bed without a murmur before he left tonight.”
“Yes, she sees that he is good,” said Mrs. Bowen.
“Oh, she sees that he’s something very much more. Mr. Waters is good.”
Miss Graham had the best of the argument, and so Mrs. Bowen did not reply.
“I feel,” continued the young girl, “as if it were almost a shame to have asked him to go to that silly dancing party with us. It seems as if we didn’t appreciate him. I think we ought to have kept him for high aesthetic occasions and historical researches.”
“Oh, I don’t think Mr. Colville was very deeply offended at being asked to go with us.”
“No,” said Imogene, with another sigh, “he didn’t seem so. I suppose there’s always an undercurrent of sadness﻿—of tragedy﻿—in everything for him.”
“I don’t suppose anything of the kind,” cried Mrs. Bowen gaily. “He’s had time enough to get over it.”
“Do people *ever* get over such things?”
“Yes﻿—men.”
“It must be because he was young, as you say. But if it had happened *now*?”
“Oh, it *couldn’t* happen now. He’s altogether too cool and calculating.”
“Do you think he’s cool and calculating?”
“No. He’s too old for a broken heart﻿—a new one.”
“Mrs. Bowen,” demanded the girl solemnly, “could *you* forgive yourself for such a thing if you had done it?”
“Yes, perfectly well, if I wasn’t in love with him.”
“But if you’d made him *think* you were?” pursued the girl breathlessly.
“If I were a flirt﻿—yes.”
“*I* couldn’t,” said Imogene, with tragic depth.
“Oh, be done with your intensities, and go to bed, Imogene,” said Mrs. Bowen, giving her a playful push.

===Chinese===

科尔夫尔带着一种自鸣得意的满足入睡——年轻人初次在社交场上度过一个成功的夜晚后，心里就盛满这种满足；而它光顾更成熟的生命，却须得有他这回这样的条件：长年流放，一朝归来。那两位可爱女人的目光一路跟进他的梦里；他知道自己准是讨了她们喜欢，孩子那戏剧性的敬意就是证据；虽说“讨得一个女人欢心”这件事足以使他幸福的年头已经过去许多年了，想讨人欢心的欲望却远没有熄灭。姑娘的眼睛像星星一样悬在他上方；他在那柔和的凝视里觉出，对她的少女心而言，他自己就是一段罗曼史——这叫他发笑；也叫他叹气。
天刚亮他肩头一阵尖锐的扭痛，醒了，便起身去享他自己的乐子：头天夜里就很受用的那些金雀花枝和松枝柴捆，亲手生一炉火，还给自己许了愿：等火烧旺了就回床上补觉，睡个懒觉。他正站在敞开的炉子前，外面一阵小铃的叮当声把他唤到窗前，他看见一列队伍刚从教堂里出来，要过广场去给某个垂死的人行终傅。本堂神父走在头里，捧着圣器里的圣体饼，身旁一个辅祭给圣体撑着一柄黄绸伞；他们后面跟着一队白袍白兜帽、遮住了脸的杂役（facchini）；他们手里的蜡烛在新亮的晨光里烧得蜡黄而没精打采；铃铛叮叮当当，凄凄惶惶。
“在这个国家，连死都死得这么有戏。”科尔夫尔想。在他身上，艺术家先被这场面的效果抓住了，人那点怜悯才替那个正在离去的可怜灵魂动起来。他为此责备自己，于是没有回床上去，而是穿了衣服，等他订的早饭那个成熟的钟点。早饭终于来了，由乔万尼（Giovanni）摊开的手掌端上来，画面煞是好看：冒气的咖啡、热牛奶、精巧圆片装的甜黄油、两只白生生的鸡蛋娇羞地卧在餐巾褶里，全组合在宽大的托盘上。它给他带来了一份额量的安慰——好饭好菜给那颗可笑的人心带来的安慰，哪怕在死亡的宅子里也不爽约。可那桩伤心事还是给他的心境镀了一层沉郁，从整个上午一直持续到将近午饭。他把这些时间用来逛教堂；可这天开局时的晴光，同前一天一样又阴了下去，下午的教堂未免太暗太冷。他去了维瑟厄的阅览室，翻了翻英文报纸——兴趣不大；又卖力地在书目里搜寻一本讲佛罗伦萨的书，给小埃菲·鲍恩：他想用自己这份心思给她母亲一个惊喜。天色向晚，他越来越按捺不住，想趁她那句“哪天来都欢迎”的话音，登门去看她。他要是年轻二十岁，准会左思右想、克制住自己；可人过了四十，凡是正当无害的纵容就不再克了；到那个岁数他已经懂得，克才是可惜，才是愚蠢。
科尔夫尔到了鲍恩太太家，客厅里半屋子客人正川流不息，来了又走；他这才想起，老桥上她给他点名的正是这一天。格雷厄姆小姐谢他头一个星期四就来了，他把“没忘掉这件事”居为功绩，宣布整个冬天每个星期四都要来。格雷厄姆小姐从俄式茶炊里给他斟了一杯茶——在有些佛罗伦萨人家，这茶炊顶替了西方文明的茶壶。科尔夫尔对着茶炊笑，也对着她笑：她俯在黄铜大壶上方，美丽的头颅花朵般微微倾侧。她穿一身乳白色驼毛的美学风格长裙，那颜色悦目，其柔软想必也悦手。
“这茶炊把气氛弄得多么屠格涅夫呀！”他说，从埃菲·鲍恩捧来的篮子里取了一块饼干，朝她扬了扬眉毛，她便加倍害羞地缩了缩。“亏你们忍得住，没叫我费奥多尔·科尔夫洛维奇。你母亲在哪儿，埃菲·鲍恩诺芙娜？”他问孩子，心里痒痒地想说一句伊莫金·格雷厄姆诺芙娜。
两人都一脸茫然，格雷厄姆小姐却说：“很遗憾，您今天见不着鲍恩太太。她头疼得厉害，把接待的事留给了埃菲和我。我们自觉很不称职，可她说这对我们有好处。”
在座有几位昨晚上见过，科尔夫尔只好同他们应酬。有位太太问他进来时可曾撞见英格尔哈特小子们（Inglehart）。
“英格尔哈特小子们？没有。什么是英格尔哈特小子们？”
“去年整个冬天他们都在这儿，刚回来。对佛罗伦萨来说，这桩事还挺叫人兴奋。”她三笔两笔给他勾出那段有趣的迁徙：二十来个年轻画家，从慕尼黑的美术学校里倾巢而出，跟着一位奇特迷人的天才——他们的名号便从他而来。“他们在慕尼黑自己办了一阵学校，后来又一齐南下意大利。画室的家什全带在身边，坐三等车旅行，走到哪儿都轰动一时，玩得也痛快。他们在佛罗伦萨是件大新闻。哪儿都去，人人喜欢。我盼着他们住下来。”
“我也盼着。”科尔夫尔说，“我倒想见识见识。”
“哎呀！”那位太太看了眼钟，“五点了！我得走了。”
别的太太们也走了，科尔夫尔上前告辞，格雷厄姆小姐却把他留住了。
“屠格涅夫是什么意思？”她追问。
“大俄罗斯小说家屠格涅夫的特质。”科尔夫尔说，看出来她没听说过此人。
“哦！”
“你该读读他。他所有的书里都升着茶炊那股好闻的气味。想让心真正碎一回，就去读《丽莎》（Lisa）。”
“您赞成书里有伤心事，我太高兴了。多少人只肯读快活的书。这是我对鲍恩太太唯一的不满。她说生活里伤心事已经这么多，就不该再放进书里。”
“啊，我看这里头有个忠孝两难。”科尔夫尔说，“我很想两边都同意。可要是鲍恩太太在场，我会提醒她：生活里既然有这么多伤心事，那也正是把它们放进书里的好理由。”
“我当然要把您这话学给她听。”
“这个嘛，书里有一定程度的快活，我并不反对；只是别推得太远——如此而已。”
这话把姑娘逗笑了，科尔夫尔受了鼓励，继续讲下去。他把黎明时分窗外所见讲给她听，描绘得有声有色，让她感受到的那份哀婉，兴许比他自己感受的还深切。“瞧，那么一桩小事跟了我一整天，给我在乔托们面前的兴高采烈降了温，叫我在契马布埃们跟前保持住得体的庄重——有几幅实在很难憋住不笑，您说是不是？”
年轻人察觉他又在说笑；他却摆出更正经的神气接着说。“您不觉得，拿这么一件事开始一天，是天大的好事吗？喏，就连在圣十字教堂（Santa Croce），在阿尔菲耶里纪念碑的荫凉里、温度计零下十度的地方，我也不比在国内哪座教堂里更快活。我想鲍恩太太会反对让那支队伍从书里的某扇窗子底下走过去；可我实在看不出这怎么就伤了读者，或是把他的兴致永久浇灭。随后自会有一场健康的反应，就像您现在在我身上看到的——这可是真人真事。”
他搅着茶，端到唇边时，内里的一阵笑抖了出来。
“是啊。”格雷厄姆小姐若有所思地说，在随后的静默里探究地望着他。可她只添了一句：“我盼着教堂里能暖和些。我几乎还没怎么看它们呢。”
“照我今天在里头的感觉，”科尔夫尔叹道，“我看教堂要到五月中旬才开始解冻。不过要是皮裘裹严实了，再带上个朋友一路唤醒你、逼你走动，免得你跌进冻死前的那种昏沉，我想现在去逛也保得了平安。您进过新圣母教堂（Santa Maria Novella）了吗？”
“没有。”格雷厄姆小姐摇摇头，那摇头表示她豁出去也要句句说真话，“连新圣母教堂也没进过。”
“那是多了不得的一座老建筑！那古怪的正立面，带着日晷，一层黑一层白的大理石，浸透了十万次落日的金红！那座精美的大门！”他拿茶杯腾出来的那只手比比画画，像艺术家那样示意形体和尺度。“再说里面！那幅契马布埃的大画，背上驮着整部名史——第一位神圣的大师画的第一位神圣的圣母，在艺术与宗教的凯旋里被人抬着走过大街！还有吉兰达约那些壁画，画着真正的佛罗伦萨面孔和身形，全挥霍在唱诗班那片永恒的暮色里——不过我猜，要是有一回放天光大亮照进去，它们就会像鸡鸣时的鬼魂一样消散！西班牙礼拜堂您千万要看；老回廊本身也是那么令人怅惘的去处。那座被取缔的修道院里如今办着一所男童学校，还有一所军校，柱廊里满是在奔跑、尖叫、大笑、快活闹腾的孩子；那比寂静还要叫人伤心。有个小淘气跑到我和领我参观的年轻修士跟前，戏弄地朝我们鞠了一躬，一低头把帽子抖落了；随后他们又哄然大笑，一哄而散，修士望着他们的背影说，说得那么温存而疲惫：‘他们在玩他们的：我们得有耐心。’那里只剩十二名修士了；其余的都散了，走了。”他把杯子递给格雷厄姆小姐再续茶。
“您不觉得，”她一面从茶炊里续着茶一面问，“修道院被取缔，是很伤心的事吗？”
“废奴也很伤心——对某些人来说。”科尔夫尔提了一句；他自己也觉得这话刁钻得不公道。
“是啊。”格雷厄姆小姐叹道。
科尔夫尔正站着搅第二杯茶，门帘一分，现出鲍恩太太在门口怅然流连的身影。她脸色苍白，可在那儿显得极美。
“啊，进来呀，鲍恩太太！”他快活地招呼她，“我不会把您出卖给别人的。一杯茶对您有好处。”
“哦，我好多了。”鲍恩太太说着，上前把手伸给他，“听见您的声音，忍不住来望一眼。”
“您太好了。”科尔夫尔感激地说；她的眼睛同他的撞了个正着，那一瞥把她脸上涨出深红。“您看，我在这儿——*我*也不知道为什么！——扮演着世交老友的角色，正尽力把年轻女士们的生活变成负担呢。”
“我想留您同我们吃顿家常晚饭。”鲍恩太太说，格雷厄姆小姐也亮起脸，热诚地赞助这份殷勤。“为什么不能呢？”
“我不知道——除非是因为我心肠软，还替您的头疼着想着想。”
“哦，早没事了。”鲍恩太太说，“那是种会挑时候的头疼——您要是害过就知道了——太阳一落就散。”
“可您明天又要害一场。”
“不，这一场下去，整整两个礼拜保您平安。”
“那您就把我推到了没有借口的境地，而我正巴不得没有呢。”科尔夫尔说。
晚饭后，鲍恩太太早早打发埃菲上床，补一补前夜的熬夜；她自己和格雷厄姆小姐却在炉火前坐到很晚，等他走了，把他从头到脚谈论了一遍。
“他真叫我捉摸不透。”格雷厄姆小姐说，“听他说话，有时你觉得他不过是个老愤世嫉俗的家伙，可忽然又冒出那么甜、那么新鲜的东西来，叫你不知如何是好。您不觉得他其实有一颗很诗人的心，其余那些全是装出来的吗？”
“我看他喜欢制造点小效果。”鲍恩太太裁断得很有分寸，“他从前就一直有点儿这样。”
“怎么，他年轻时就这样？”
“我并不觉得他现在很老。”
“当然不老。我是说您从前认得他的时候。”格雷厄姆小姐手里做着针线；鲍恩太太呢——她那种活计连装装样子都不曾有过——手里只有那柄羽毛扇屏。
“同你比，他是老了，伊莫金；不过你往后再活活看，会发现你的同龄人永远是年轻的。科尔夫尔先生大有长进。他从前怕人怕得厉害，却硬端着一副大胆的门面，如今那门面像是成了他的第二天性。”
“我喜欢这副门面。”格雷厄姆小姐对着针脚说。
“是啊；可我疑心他心底里还是怕人。他从前非常多愁善感，开口闭口罗斯金。我想他要不是罗斯金讲得那么多，珍妮·米尔伯里（Jenny Milbury）也许会待他好些。他那时候着实一副学究气。”
“哦，科尔夫尔先生会学究气，我想不出！”
“他大有长进。他从前穿着上相当邋遢；反正您总知道，大多数美国绅士穿着有多邋遢。我想这也叫她对他生了反感。”
“他现在可不邋遢了。”格雷厄姆小姐提了一句。
“哦不；他现在相当时髦阔气。”鲍恩太太说，那腔调的雅致把形容词里的俚气全洗掉了。
“这个嘛，”格雷厄姆小姐说，“那以后您怎么还受得了她，我真不懂。太恶劣了。”
“她要是发现自己不爱他，那么同他断掉，总好过嫁给他。那一种，”鲍恩太太说，带着她少有的深情，“可要糟上一千倍。”
“是，可她本该早点弄明白，不该把他引到那么远。”
“有时候姑娘们弄不明白。她们不认识自己；不是恋爱，也当是恋爱。她性子很冲动，这事当然也叫她得意；他又是那么有学问。可到头来她发现自己受不了了，只好照直告诉他。”
“她说过为什么不爱他吗？”
“没有；我想她说不出。我只记得她说过一句：他这个人‘成分太杂’。”
“她这话*到底*是什么意思？”
“我也说不确切。”
“您觉得他不真诚？”
“哦不。兴许她的意思是，他用心不专。”
“薄情？”
“不。在她那桩事上，他肯定不薄情。”
“优柔寡断？”
“对她他可够决断的——到最后。”
伊莫金放弃了这条无望的追问。“一个男人怎么能受得了这种事？”她叹道。
“他承受得非常高尚。那正是这件事里最好的部分；他把它接得极其体贴。她给我看过他的信。里面没有一个字、一丝暗示的责备；他好像只为她替他难过这一点放心不下。当然，他免不了流露出自己的懊丧——自己一片殷勤、原来于她是苦事；可那份流露也是体贴的。是啊，那是一封非常大度的信。”
“她拿给人看，这太不像话了。”
“是欠妥。可那封信，哪个姑娘拿出来都算拿得出手。”
“哦，她总不至于是为了满足虚荣才这么做的！”
“姑娘家是非常古怪的，亲爱的。”鲍恩太太说，仿佛这事实是一条抽象定理。她对着扇屏的平面对它冥想，格雷厄姆小姐则默默走针。
还是后者先开了口。“您觉得他被这事击垮得很厉害吗？”
“这谁说得准。他后来就去了西部，从此待在那儿。我想，要不是出了这一桩，他的一生会完全两样。他很有才。我那时总以为，往后总会以某种方式听到他的消息。”
“哼，这真是没心没肺、不知羞耻的事！我不懂您怎么能说得这么轻描淡写，鲍恩太太。这叫我比从前更恨种种卖弄风情、打情骂俏。您想想，这毁了他的一生！”
“哦，他那时还年轻，熬得过去。说到底，不过是少男少女。”
“少男少女！他们那时多大？”
“他该是二十三四岁，她二十岁。”
“*正是我*这个年纪！这也叫少女？”
“她的年纪，足够明白自己在做什么了。”鲍恩太太说得公道。
伊莫金靠回椅背，把针带着线全长地抽出来，然后让手落下去。“我不知道。我好像永远长不大，永远只是个孩子。是啊，想想年轻男人说话的那副样子，他们确实还是孩子。他们怎么就不能像科尔夫尔先生那样说话？我真希望我也能像他那样说话。他一开口，你就忘了他又老又平常。”
她垂着眼皮，心不在焉地检阅着自己的针脚；鲍恩太太望着她，眼神里含着恼怒、不耐、怨怼——每一样都精炼到了最后一重——不等格雷厄姆小姐抬头，她已把这些从脸上驱逐干净。小姐抬起头，笑着说：“看埃菲迷他迷成那样，多有意思！她一眨眼也舍不得离开他，满屋子跟着他转，生怕漏听他一个字。今晚他还没走她就乖乖上了床，一声没吭，真是英勇。”
“是啊，她看得出他是个好人。”鲍恩太太说。
“哦，她看出来的可远不止是好人。沃特斯先生才是好人。”
这场辩论格雷厄姆小姐占了上风，于是鲍恩太太没有应声。
“我心里，”年轻姑娘接着说，“几乎觉得惭愧，竟请他陪我们去参加那个傻兮兮的舞会。好像我们不知道珍惜他似的。我想我们本该把他留着，专供隆重的美学场合和历史考据之用。”
“哦，我想科尔夫尔先生被我们邀去，未必见怪多深。”
“是啊，”伊莫金又叹了一声，“他看上去也不像见怪。我想，在他那里，万事底下总潜着一股哀愁——一股悲剧。”
“我才不作任何这种想头。”鲍恩太太扬声道，“他早有工夫把它熬过去了。”
“人*到底*熬得过去这种事吗？”
“熬得过去——男人。”
“那一定是如您说的，他那时年轻。可这事要是出在*如今*呢？”
“哦，*如今*根本出不了这种事。他现在冷静、精明得过头了。”
“您觉得他冷静精明？”
“不。他这个岁数，不宜再碎一颗心了——一颗新的。”
“鲍恩太太，”姑娘郑重地质问，“这种事要是您做下的，*您*能饶恕自己吗？”
“能，完全能饶恕——只要我没爱上他。”
“可要是您叫他*以为*您爱上了呢？”姑娘屏着气追问。
“我要是个卖弄风情的女人——那也能。”
“*我*可不能。”伊莫金说，语气悲剧得深。
“哦，收起你那套激烈吧，上床去，伊莫金。”鲍恩太太说着，推了她一把，闹着玩儿的。

## VI / 六
===Original===

It was so long since Colville had been at a dancing party that Mrs. Bowen’s offer to take him to Madame Uccelli’s had first struck his sense of the ludicrous. Then it had begun to flatter him; it implied that he was still young enough to dance if he would, though he had stipulated that they were not to expect anything of the kind from him. He liked also the notion of being seen and accepted in Florentine society as the old friend of Mrs. Bowen’s, for he had not been long in discovering that her position in Florence was, among the foreign residents, rather authoritative. She was one of the very few Americans who were asked to Italian houses, and Italian houses lying even beyond the neutral ground of English-speaking intermarriages. She was not, of course, asked to the great Princess Strozzi ball, where the Florentine nobility appeared in the medieval pomp﻿—the veritable costumes﻿—of their ancestors; only a rich American banking family went, and their distinction was spoken of under the breath; but any glory short of this was within Mrs. Bowen’s reach. So an old lady who possessed herself of Colville the night before had told him, celebrating Mrs. Bowen at length, and boasting of her acceptance among the best English residents, who, next after the natives, seem to constitute the social ambition of Americans living in Italian cities.
It interested him to find that some geographical distinctions which are fading at home had quite disappeared in Florence. When he was there before, people from quite small towns in the East had made pretty Lina Ridgely and her friend feel the disadvantage of having come from the Western side of an imaginary line; he had himself been at the pains always to let people know, at the American watering-places where he spent his vacations, that though presently from Des Vaches, Indiana, he was really born in Rhode Island; but in Florence it was not at all necessary. He found in Mrs. Bowen’s house people from Denver, Chicago, St. Louis, Boston, New York, and Baltimore, all meeting as of apparently the same civilisation, and whether Mrs. Bowen’s own origin was, like that of the Etruscan cities, lost in the mists of antiquity, or whether she had sufficiently atoned for the error of her birth by subsequent residence in the national capital and prolonged sojourn in New York, it seemed certainly not to be remembered against her among her Eastern acquaintance. The time had been when the fact that Miss Graham came from Buffalo would have gone far to class her with the animal from which her native city had taken its name; but now it made no difference, unless it was a difference in her favour. The English spoke with the same vague respect of Buffalo and of Philadelphia; and to a family of real Bostonians Colville had the courage to say simply that he lived in Des Vaches, and not to seek to palliate the truth in any sort. If he wished to prevaricate at all, it was rather to attribute himself to Mrs. Bowen’s city in Ohio.
She and Miss Graham called for him with her carriage the next night, when it was time to go to Madame Uccelli’s.
“This gives me a very patronised and effeminate feeling,” said Colville, getting into the odorous dark of the carriage, and settling himself upon the front seat with a skill inspired by his anxiety not to tear any of the silken spreading white wraps that inundated the whole interior. “Being come for by ladies!” They both gave some nervous joyful laughs, as they found his hand in the obscurity, and left the sense of a gloved pressure upon it. “Is this the way you used to do in Vesprucius, Mrs. Bowen?”
“Oh no, indeed!” she answered. “The young gentlemen used to find out whether I was going, and came for me with a hack, and generally, if the weather was good, we walked home.”
“That’s the way we still do in Des Vaches. Sometimes, as a tremendous joke, the ladies come for us in leap-year. How do you go to balls in Buffalo, Miss Graham? Or, no; I withdraw the embarrassing question.” Some gleams from the street lamps, as they drove along, struck in through the carriage windows, and flitted over the ladies’ faces and were gone again. “Ah! this is very trying. Couldn’t you stop him at the next corner, and let me see how radiant you ladies really are? I may be in very great danger; I’d like to know just how much.”
“It wouldn’t be of any use,” cried the young girl gaily. “We’re all wrapped up, and you couldn’t form any idea of us. You must wait, and let us burst upon you when we come out of the dressing-room at Madame Uccelli’s.”
“But then it may be too late,” he urged. “Is it very far?”
“Yes,” said Mrs. Bowen. “It’s ridiculously far. It’s outside the Roman Gate. I don’t see why people live at that distance.”
“In order to give the friends you bring the more pleasure of your company, Mrs. Bowen.”
“Ah! that’s very well. But you’re not logical.”
